ANL/NSE-25/34 Rev. 1

 Software Validation Work With
 The ZPPR-15 Data

by
Gerardo Aliberti
Richard M. Lell
Zhaopeng Zhong
Micheal A. Smith
Nuclear Science and Engineering Division
Argonne National Laboratory

SEP 2026

 ABSTRACT
 The analysis activities for fast reactors involve using many different pieces of software
that are relied upon for their predictive capabilities. For this software to be considered reliable,
documented proof that the predictions of the software are accurate is required. In this
manuscript, the validation work that covers some of the Argonne software used in fast reactor
design activities is discussed and displayed. This validation work includes neutron and gamma
flux distributions, reaction rate distributions, and reactivity worth.
 In an ideal world, a reactor development program would have access to a comprehensive
set of experimental facilities to help inform the design aspects of the reactor itself. While
thermal-hydraulics experiments, and to a limited degree mechanical experiments, can be carried
out today for validation needs, neutronics related experimental facilities are rather impractical
because of the lack of experimental facilities. Given the desired time table for construction of
new reactors, the reconstitution or creation of new neutronic experimental facilities is untenable
and thus those reactor development programs must rely upon any available experimental
measurements that are qualitatively similar to the design. While a methodology has been
proposed to assess the similarity between the past experimental measurements and the reactor
itself, that aspect is beyond the scope of this manuscript. In this manuscript, the focus is entirely
placed on the analysis results for a series of experiments carried out at the ZPPR facility in
Idaho in the mid-1980s. In this regard, this manuscript only shows the validation of the stated
neutronics software for specific loadings of the ZPPR reactor.
 Because of the fuel form, its proposed enrichment, and the material content of the
reactor core, the ZPPR-15 experiments were identified as potential validation data for the
reactor. The ZPPR-15 experiments were intended as mockups of a 330 MWe Integral Fast
Reactor program which was a follow on program to the Clinch River Breeder Reactor. In the
ZPPR-15 series of experiments, measurements of the neutron spectrum, control rod worth,
sodium void worth, foil reaction rate distributions, Doppler worth of heated samples, gamma
dose, and axial expansion worth were all carried out and published. In many cases, these
reactivity coefficients are good candidates to validate the reactivity coefficient calculation
scheme used by the analysis software and included in the safety analysis activities of fast reactor
development projects today. This manuscript discusses the modeling methodology and
accuracy of the calculated experimental results using the LANL software MCNP and the ANL
software package ARC (Argonne Reactor Codes).
 As will be shown, for many of the experimental measurements, the two software
packages are found to be good predictive analysis tools for those experiments. In other cases,
problems with the analysis methodology or underlying cross section data are exposed which
indicates where predictive analysis is not as reliable. Finally, in some of the measurements the
conclusion is reached that the experimental measurement cannot be reproduced with the
analysis software as it is simply too difficult.

1. Introduction
 Argonne National Laboratory (ANL) has maintained its own fast reactor analysis
software package since the early 1960s to meet its reactor design and operation missions. Figure
1 shows the present connections of the Argonne Reactor Computation (ARC) code system [1],[2]
where the SAS4A component [3] is not considered part of this work. This report details
validation work that demonstrates the fidelity of the software components listed in Figure 1 for
metal fueled fast reactor modeling. This is a nontrivial task as this connected set of codes is
multi-disciplinary including neutronics (MC2 [4], DIF3D [5][6][7][8], REBUS [9], PERSENT
[10], GAMSOR [11], RCT [12]), thermal analysis (SUPERENERGY [13] and DASSH [14]),
and mechanics (NUBOW [15]). Only some of these codes can be validated using the ZPPR data.

 Figure 1. Argonne Reactor Computation Code System
 Because of the availability of measured data, the validation work on the neutronics
components was chosen as the starting point. It is also relevant to point out that all of the
connected components in Figure 1 flow from the neutronics components to thermal analysis,
mechanics, and then safety analysis. While the outcome of those analysis activities can feed back
into the neutronics as changes to the design requirements, the validation work naturally flows
from neutronics as it will bound the downstream components and, combined with reactor design
features, dictate the focus of the analysis in the later connected components.

 1

 The U.S. does not have any active experimental facilities that can neutronically mockup
large commercial fast reactors. However, in the past, the U.S. had extensive experimental
facilities for fast reactor neutronics including several Zero Power Reactors (ZPR-3, ZPR-6, ZPR-
9), the Zero Power Plutonium Reactor (ZPPR) [16], EBR-II [17], and FFTF [18]. Combined,
these reactors provide a large volume of usable validation data although the operating reactors
EBR-II and FFTF come with considerable loading and operating uncertainties and the ZPR and
ZPPR experiments typically have features that are not present in most fast reactor designs today
(blankets, axially symmetric control rods, etc…).
 To begin the validation work, the experiments that are most relevant to current fast
reactor concepts should be identified. In that regard, the ZPPR-15 experiments carried out at
ANL during the 1980s work best because the fuel form is closest, there is a considerable amount
of processed data available for use, existing staff are already familiar with the experimental
machine and measurements. Because of previous collaborative work, many of the ZPPR-15
loadings of interest have already been processed into MCNP and ARC models, and a full
uncertainty quantification has already been completed for many of the ZPPR-15 measurements.
 This manuscript covers the recent analysis work on the ZPPR-15 experiments and
compares it with the experimental results. Because the previous work on ZPPR-15 was split
between multiple staff and carried out over multiple years, the recent work focused on repeating
the previous analysis efforts such that all loadings are modeled consistently, better
documentation of the analysis and modeling work is created, and all of the inputs are archived
for later re-use. This work might have to be repeated as software or data updates are made. With
respect to the ARC software components, this manuscript focuses on validation of the MC 2,
DIF3D, and GAMSOR parts of Figure 1 using the ZPPR-15 experimental data.
2. ZPPR-15 Experimental Data
 The ZPPR-15 experiments [16] were mockups of a 330 MWe Integral Fast Reactor (IFR).
The ZPPR-15 assembly consisted of a clean, two zone, approximately circular core surrounded
by a thin depleted uranium (DU) blanket with sodium (Na) cooling and a thick stainless steel
reflector. The ZPPR-15 program was conducted in four phases: A, B, C, and D. Each phase was
marked by a particular composition of the reference assembly, with the last three being
representative of the three stages of the Integral Fast Reactor (IFR) fuel cycle.

2.1 ZPPR-15 Loading Details
 The ZPPR fast critical facility was a horizontal split-table type machine consisting of a
large, cast-steel bed supporting two tables, one stationary and the other movable. Figure 2 shows
a picture taken of the ZPPR facility during one of the later ZPPR experiments. The desired
average composition for a given core was achieved by loading the reactor matrix with drawers
containing rectangular plates of different materials such as Pu, depleted or natural U, stainless
steel and Na. A specific plate loading pattern in a drawer is called “drawer master”. Figure 2 also
shows a historical photo of plates loaded into a drawer. In the drawers of both stationary and
movable halves of the ZPPR-15 loadings, the core zone extends from 0 to 18.03-in, the blanket
extends from 18.03 to 31.03-in, the first part of the axial reflector extends from 31.03 to 36.252-
in and the second part of the axial reflector extends from 36.252 to 42.252-in. Each matrix tube
position extends to 60-in, and excluding control rod drive lines and experiment cables, they were
all empty beyond 42.252-in. The 0.03-in offset (e.g. 18.03) is due to the front plate of the drawer

 2

which is 0.03-in thick. The drawer used to insert the core and blanket materials was 36.252-in
long with the remaining components of the axial reflector simply placed into the matrix (i.e. no
back drawers were used). In ZPPR-15C and ZPPR-15D loadings, the cladding on the HEU plates
gave them non-standard dimensions and caused them to extend slightly into the axial blanket
zone (see Appendix C). The axial section of the matrix tubes beyond 42.252-in are empty and
are typically discarded in deterministic computational models but are included in the Monte
Carlo models solved with MCNP [19].

 Figure 2. The ZPPR Facility (Left) and a Typical Drawer Loaded with Plates (Right)

 The specification of which drawer master was in each matrix position is known as the
“matrix loading map.” A matrix position in ZPPR-15 is specified by three parameters: matrix
half (1 for stationary or 2 for movable), row number (starting from the top with number 11), and
column number (starting from the left with number 11 and looking from the movable half
towards the stationary half). In the ZPPR matrix numbering convention, each matrix position is
identified by two numbers separated by a hyphen, e.g., 133-27. In this example, 133 refers to
row 33 in half 1, and 27 refers to column 27. Row 33, column 27 in half 2 would be listed as
233-27. Because the column numbers for both halves start from the same side of the machine,
the row and column numbers in the stationary and movable tables of the machine align when the
tables are brought together. For example, the matrix positions designated as row 33, column 27
(i.e., 133-27 and 233-27) are directly aligned when the movable table touches the stationary
table.

 3

 Because of significant uncertainties in the Zr cross sections (at the time) and the relative
lack of integral data for fast systems with a major Zr component, only Pu, depleted uranium
(DU), stainless steel, and sodium (Na) were included in the first phase (ZPPR-15A) of the
program. This allowed examination of the effect of removing oxygen from a typical liquid-metal
fast breeder reactor (LMFBR) design of the time which used oxide fuel. In the second phase,
ZPPR-15B, zirconium was added to study the impact of the proposed Zr-alloyed metallic fuel.
In both ZPPR-15A and ZPPR-15B, the inner core only consisted of drawers filled with a single
column of Pu fuel plates while the outer core region had a mixture of single and double column
fueled drawers. In ZPPR-15C, roughly half of the core volume was fueled by enriched U to
simulate an IFR transition composition. In phase 15D of the program, the enriched U component
was increased to 90% of the core volume, simulating a near-beginning-of-life composition.
Approximately 10% of the drawers in the outer core of ZPPR-15D contained Pu-U-Mo fuel to
provide a distributed neutron source for subcritical measurements.

2.2 Identification of the ZPPR-15 Experiments of Interest
 Because the ZPPR experiments do not have meaningful burnup data, the main focus for
their use is on safety analysis. In operating fast spectrum reactors, the reactivity feedbacks that
dictate the stability of the reactor during transients are typically small. Thus, having highly
accurate measurements of small perturbations and being able to predict them with the modeling
software is key to the accuracy of the software used to model the transients. This is therefore a
role of the ZPPR-15 data that can be exploited for ongoing fast reactor projects.
 The ZPPR-15 experimental data is presently maintained by both INL and ANL in both
paper and electronic forms. The first task was to identify which ZPPR-15 experimental loadings
and measurements were of use for fast reactor development projects. Table 1 shows the list of
ZPPR-15 loadings and relative measurements that were selected for use in the current validation
effort with respect to the ARC software.
 With each ZPPR-15 phase, the critical loading is used to assess material and
compositional uncertainties associated with the ZPPR-15 experiments along with providing a
base check of the accuracy of criticality predictions from the ARC (and Monte Carlo) software.
The control rod and sodium void worth measurements have relatively obvious uses in fast reactor
analysis project for safety analysis and thus all available measurements were included in the
work scope. The remaining measurements and their use can be identified as:
 1) Neutron Spectrum: This measurement provides support for predicting the radiation
 damage done by fast neutrons as it is a direct measure of the fast neutron flux magnitude.
 It also can be used to quantify the accuracy of flux and reaction rate calculations.
 2) Boron 10 Counter: This measurement can give better insight on the accuracy of control
 rod worth beyond the large scale loading changes that the regular control rod
 measurements entail.
 3) Sodium Manometer: This measurement, along with the smaller sodium void worth
 measurements, can be used as validation data for sodium density worth.
 4) Foils: These are spatial distributions of relative reaction rate measurements. Because they
 extend from the interior of the core to the outer shielding regions, they provide excellent
 data on the accuracy of flux and cross section predictions throughout the reactor system.

 4

 They can also be used to support fuel depletion validation work although they lack the
 necessary details to be used as fuel depletion validation.
 5) Pu242 and Np237 Counter: This measurement provides additional reaction rate
 measurements on the specified isotopes. It has no use for reactivity measurements but it
 could be useful for the follow-on fuel depletion validation work.
 6) Doppler Sample: This measurement provides small reactivity worth measurements
 associated with temperature changes in a heated sample. It poses one of the few
 temperature specific measurements available and has direct implications on the
 temperature reactivity worth used in safety analysis.
 7) Axial Expansion: This measurement provides measured data on the worth associated
 with assumed mechanical changes to an assembly due to movement. Combined with the
 Doppler sample measurements, this provides validation support for the axial expansion
 reactivity worth.
 8) Gamma TLD: This measurement is primarily focused on the GAMSOR validation work
 as it provides gamma associated reaction rate distributions in the domain. While the
 measurement uncertainty is relatively high, it is the best available measured data on the
 subject where gamma heating accounts for more than 5% of the total power in most fast
 reactors.
 Most of these experiments contribute to the validation of the MC 2 cross section
generation and DIF3D modeling accuracy. Some were specifically chosen to support GAMSOR
validation and others to support the follow-on work using measured EBR-II and FFTF data. As
can be seen in Table 1, a considerable number of loadings were completed because of the already
processed experimental data and availability of the loading details.
 The layout of the stationary half of each loading listed in Table 1 can be found in
Appendix A. Appendix B gives the full list of drawers that were used in each loading.

 5

2.3 ZPPR-15 Deterministic Methodology
 In the ANL modeling software, each drawer of ZPPR-15, a physical example of which
was shown earlier in Figure 2, is defined as input to the BLDVIM software [20] with respect to
plates, drawer, and matrix tube. Figure 3 shows the computer model of the physical drawer of
ZPR-6/7 where rather minor simplifications in geometry are made (e.g. removal of holes in
drawer ends, removal of drawer tabs and matrix tube notches, and a simple representation of a
spring).

 Figure 3. Computer Model of a ZPR-6/7 Drawer Loaded with Plates
 The Monte Carlo models are based upon this slightly simplified model of the ZPPR-15
drawer and thus provide the most accurate geometrical model possible with modern neutronics
analysis tools. The ARC software shown earlier in Figure 1 is not able to computationally process
and model ZPPR experiments to the same geometrical fidelity as the Monte Carlo codes and thus
some level of simplification is required. DIF3D is based upon the multi-group methodology
where 33, 70, 116, and 230 group structures are typically used for fast spectrum reactor analysis
today. Using more energy groups should improve the modeling accuracy, but because DIF3D is
not multi-processor capable, the calculations are still restricted by computer memory and
computational effort.
 DIF3D is also based upon a structured grid and while spatial homogenization is not
required for ZPPR problems, using spatial homogenization is common for modern reactor
analysis. Performing deterministic analysis of a ZPPR problem adds an additional layer of
difficulty because the monolithic plate geometry repeated in each drawer requires additional care
to be taken during the cross section generation process. The primary issue is that highly enriched
plates are separated from the depleted uranium ones by a variety of simulated structural and
coolant materials which makes energy self-shielding different from conventional fast reactor
analysis. This can be seen in Figure 4 where the Pu plate is sandwiched between two iron oxide
plates. (The example in Figure 3 and Figure 4 was a drawer from ZPR-6/7 which simulated an
oxide-fueled fast reactor. There were no iron oxide or uranium oxide plates in ZPPR-15.)

 7

 Figure 4. Example Cross Section of a ZPR-6/7 Drawer With Plates and a MC 2 1D model
 To overcome these problems the lattice capabilities added to the MC 2 code that
specifically target the slab geometry issues associated with ZPR and ZPPR calculations are used.
Because of the recent upgrades to the DIF3D-VARIANT code, a “drawer” homogenization
model can be used where previous deterministic approaches would require the creation of an
equivalent RZ model and perturbations on that geometry. In the “drawer” homogenized model,
each drawer is axially smeared into a few homogeneous regions according to the composition
heterogeneity as outlined in Figure 5.

 Figure 5. DIF3D-VARIANT Model (lower) of a Core Drawer (upper)
 From here MC2-3 is used to perform individual drawer lattice calculations and build a
DIF3D input geometry file for the homogenized model. This process is carried out with the
BuildZPRModel code [20] developed at ANL specifically for this purpose. For each ZPPR
problem, homogenization instructions for each drawer must be given along with any drawer-
wise cross section substitutions in the stationary and movable sides of the experiments (e.g. use
cross sections generated for drawer type 101 in drawer type 102). The full ZPPR model can be
truncated to remove the empty matrix tube as desired to reduce the computational effort of the
final model. In many cases the plates in each drawer are not exactly aligned axially or the drawers
contain mixes of control rod, thermocouple, and a partial core drawer. These exceptions are all
handled with additional input indicating how they are to be treated and whether cross section
data is to be obtained from adjacent drawers.
 The output of BuildZPRModel is a series of MC 2-3 inputs that generate the cross section
data and a drawer homogenized DIF3D input. Figure 6 shows an example of DIF3D geometry
representation of the critical loadings of ZPPR-15. It is important to note that in all of the

 8

calculations performed in this manuscript, the empty matrix tubes surrounding the loaded matrix
tubes are removed to reduce the computational effort. This has a near trivial impact upon the
system keff and flux solution.

 ZPPR-15A ZPPR-15B

 ZPPR-15C ZPPR-15D
 Figure 6. DIF3D Input Geometry Examples for All Phases of ZPPR-15
2.4 Consistency Setup for ZPPR-15 Deterministic Calculations
 The BuildZPRmodel software has three different methodologies for cross section
generation. The first methodology, termed uncoupled (UC), assumes that the drawers are not
connected and that independent cross section generation can be carried out either by the fission
source (core drawers) within each drawer or a volumetric fission spectrum source (blanket,
reflector, shielding drawers). This approach is the simplest and generally works very well for
ZPPR and ZPR calculations. The second methodology, termed leakage coupled (LC), replaces
the volumetric fission spectrum source in the non-core drawers with a leakage based source
derived from the inner core regions. The blankets, reflectors, and shielding drawers are also
coupled together by computing a new leakage source from the innermost region (blanket) to use
in the next outer region (reflector). The third methodology (RZ) uses a two-dimensional R-Z
model of the ZPPR geometry in MC2 to produce cross sections for the blanket, reflector, and
shielding drawers. In this case, MC2 uses TWODANT to produce a 1041 group flux solution and
thus get better region-wise cross section data in the non-core regions of the domain. The
advantage of the RZ approach is that fewer energy groups are needed in the DIF3D calculations
to obtain the same answer as the UC and LC approaches. In effect, using the RZ approach allows

 9

the whole core calculation to be approximately processed at the 1041 group level before doing
the real 3D calculation. All of the calculations in this manuscript were performed using ENDF/B-
VII.0 data.
 The RZ approach is consistent with the RZ modeling approach used for most fast reactor
analysis today and thus a direct one-to-one comparison capability for ZPPR calculations is
possible. However, the radial and axial blanket regions in ZPPR-15 cause the UC, LC, and RZ
methodologies to all produce rather consistent results for the typical energy group structures. As
a consequence, all of the calculations are performed with the UC approach as it best exposes the
energy dependence of the DIF3D modeling.
 The preceding modeling methodology all relies upon the concept that the drawers form
a monolithic structure over a “large” portion of the domain. Looking at ZPPR-15A in Figure 6,
one can see that the active core is broken into an inner core (solid red) and outer core (red
checkered pattern), a depleted uranium blanket (gray color), and radial reflector (light green
region). In the inner core one can consider it to be large and the thus repeating plate pattern
justifies the assumption of an infinite lattice behavior. In the outer core region, the same
assumption can be valid so long as the drawer loadings are similar. For 15A and 15D, this
happens to be the case as the drawers loaded into the outer core are very similar. For 15B this is
not the case as the region consists of a mixture of single and double fuel column drawers and the
resulting bias was not studied. For 15C, the entire core region has a scatter pattern due to the use
of different fuel types (enriched uranium and plutonium). For 15C, the loading pattern does not
really define a “large” region and a numerical study indicated that this has at least a 100 pcm
bias in the criticality predictions of DIF3D. Because the existing enriched uranium drawers
lattice calculations in MC2 are causing a +600 pcm bias, the loading pattern issues for 15C are
rather obscured and no action was taken to resolve it at this time.
 In addition to the cross section generation, the space-angle approximations in DIF3D-
VARIANT must also be considered. DIF3D-VARIANT is based upon an even-parity
formulation of the transport equation and spatially uses a hybrid finite element method. The finite
element method uses high order polynomials internal to each mesh to handle the gradients in the
solution near material boundaries and low order polynomials to connect the meshes (i.e.
boundary conditions). For the angular treatment, DIF3D-VARIANT uses a spherical harmonics
expansion. In several of the ZPPR models discussed in this document, results are given to display
the space-angle-energy convergence.
 An additional consistency problem to deal with is that many ZPPR-15 worth calculations
are based upon the differences between criticality of two loadings. With regard to deterministic
modeling, the same cross section generation methodology should be used for both of the
loadings. As an example, Loading 17 and Loading 19 are two sodium void cases of ZPPR-15A
while Loading 16 is the reference sub-critical loading. The sodium void worth is typically
reported as the worth between two consecutive loadings (e.g. Loading 16 to 17 or 17 to 18). In
these cases, the deterministic methodology has to be identical to prevent additional error from
being introduced from the cross section generation methodology or meshing changes. Because
of the way the sodium void results were reported, and the desire to minimize the number of
different deterministic models that have to be constructed, all critical loading based worth
calculations are taken with respect to a reference critical or sub-critical loading as available. For
loading 17 and 19, this means the cross section generation methodology used in loading 16 is

 10

modified to match that which is required to model loading 17 and loading 19 and the meshing
applied between all three loadings is identical.
 In Table 2, the impact of the different cross section generation schemes applied to loading
16 of ZPPR-15A is displayed showing a net 90 pcm change in the criticality prediction for
loading 16 just from the cross section methodology.
 Table 2. Cross Section Generation Methodology Impact for DIF3D-VARIANT+MC 2
 Measurement DIF3D error
 ZPPR-15A
 keff σ keff (pcm)
                       Loading 16                           0.99661 0.00014    0.99571        -91
Loading 16 Using the Cross Section Scheme from Loading 17 0.99630 -31
Loading 16 Using the Cross Section Scheme from Loading 19 0.99661 0

 Assuming a measured sodium void worth was reported between loadings 16 and 17,
without correcting the loading 16 reference state in the deterministic modeling, the -31 pcm
result in Table 2 would appear as an additional contribution to the sodium void worth when it is
in fact just a modeling issue, similar to using different spatial or energy group approximations in
the two deterministic calculations. In this manuscript, the reference critical and sub-critical
loading of each criticality based measurement (sodium void worth, control rod worth, etc…) are
re-calculated such that the spatial cross section methodology is identical to that used in the
perturbed state to eliminate this aspect of error in the deterministic calculations unless otherwise
noted. This approach is not faithful to the actual measurement itself and should involve merging
the measurement errors from multiple intermediate measurements between two loadings (i.e. 16
to 19) when computing the reference result which is ignored in the deterministic approach.
2.5 Mapping Specifications
 A crucial phase in the preparation of the BuildZPRModel inputs is deciding how to
generate cross sections for each loading. As discussed above, BuildZPRModel allows users to
assign microscopic cross-sections generated for one drawer (termed the “representative” drawer)
to another (target) drawer in the loading. With this approach, the macroscopic cross-sections for
the target drawer are obtained by multiplying the correct homogenized target drawer number
densities with the microscopic cross sections from the representative drawer.
 The criteria for the selection of the representative drawers should be based both on
geometric (similarity in drawer loading patterns and materials) and neutron spectra
considerations. For instance, control rod drawers are typically mapped to adjacent core drawers
such that the cross sections are processed with the most appropriate neutron spectra (cross section
generation based upon lattice calculations with control rods is ill-advised). Given this, attention
to the geometric similarity with the representative drawer is very important. In many ZPPR-15
loadings there are usually many sizes available for a given plate material and a limited supply of
any particular size. As a consequence, there were often several drawer masters (DM) that had
essentially the same composition but loaded using different plate sizes. Of particular concern for
mapping is that in loadings of ZPPR-15C and ZPPR-15D, there are drawers with thicker and/or
thinner HEU plates. In these situations, it is important that the mapping be made between similar
drawers.

 11

 As an example, in the ZPPR-15C critical loading (i.e., L166), drawers 915, 916, 917,
918, 919, 920 and drawers 923, 924 use a thick HEU plate (see Appendix C). It was found that
mapping drawers 915, 916, 917, 918, 919, 920 to drawer 905 (thin HEU plate) yields a +100
pcm bias in keff relative to the case where those drawers are mapped to drawer 915. Similarly,
mapping drawers 923 and 924 to drawer 921 (a drawer with a thin uranium plate) causes a +50
pcm bias with respect to mapping them to drawer 923 (correct HEU plate thickness).
 To guarantee consistency, all of the drawers loaded in all of the ZPPR-15 experiments
were analyzed before any calculations started. A single cross section generation plan was devised
for all ZPPR-15 experiments. Appendix C shows the mapping specifications that were used in
BuildZPRModel for the cross section generation of all drawers of the ZPPR-15 loadings selected
in the present study. To facilitate the understanding of the adopted mapping specifications,
Appendix C also provides a schematic view of the loading pattern of each drawer as generated
by BuildZPRModel. It is worth noting that this cross section generation plan had to be adjusted
for the analysis of some loadings as the chosen representative drawer was not present in the
loading. These details are given at the end of Appendix C.
 From the graphic representation of each drawer in Appendix C, one can find the inclusion
of an aluminum plate in numerous drawers (typically radial reflector or thermocouple drawers)
in the last axial position. This aluminum is not present in the actual loading records and was
added to the empty matrix tube region in an earlier approaches because of a methodological
limitation of the DIF3D-VARIANT code with regard to void or low density meshes. To
guarantee convergence of the steady state solution, a 1x1 inch aluminum plate was artificially
added to those drawers which have fully empty matrix tubes that need to be modeled.
 Between the original work done on this project in 2019 and the current revision, an
updated version of BuildZPRModel was created which introduces a manufactured pure
absorbing isotope called FAKEPA which is added in place of the aluminum plates. The pictures
in Appendix C are not updated as they clearly indicate where this new isotope is introduced. In
this report, the FAKEPA isotope is added to the EMT regions for all modeled loadings of ZPPR-
15 unless otherwise noted and there are no aluminum plates included in any of the results detailed
in this report as was done in previous revisions.
 The manufactured FAKEPA cross section stabilizes the DIF3D-VARIANT iterative
methodology and thereby minimizes the impact that this geometric alteration has on the actual
results. It is worth noting that the manufactured cross section approach attempts to achieve an
effective minimal multi-group macroscopic absorption cross section of 0.02 cm -1 in any given
EMT region which has a near trivial impact on the keff in the problem given where it is being
added. With the implementation, the magnitude of the manufactured cross section inclusion can
be adjusted by the user to obtain keff at multiple points and thereby reliably extrapolate the 0.0
inclusion of the correction on the system. The impact of the aluminum plate across many ZPR
and ZPPR problems has been observed to be between 0-5 pcm. Because the amount of aluminum
plate inclusion, or FAKEPA inclusion, changes for each loading, its impact on k eff is also variable
and thus we only display the results for FAKEPA for Loading 88 in Table 3. The default
inclusion of the FAKEPA isotope is 1.0 and, as can be seen, the impact upon the eigenvalue is
rather minimal in the critical reference loading for ZPPR-15B. While divergence did not occur
when the atom density was dropped from the default of 1.0, the change in k eff is beyond the
convergence limit and thus pushing the atom density to zero is mostly irrelevant as it is already

 12

obvious that including the FAKEPA isotope has a negligible impact on keff and, by extension,
the flux solution. Compared with the 0-5 pcm effect of adding the aluminum plate, clearly the
FAKEPA approach is much wiser.
 Table 3. ZPPR-15B Loading 88 Impact of different magnitudes of FAKEPA on k eff.
 FAKEPA Atom Density keff
                                           2.0         0.99888 63
                                           1.5         0.99888 64
                                           1.0         0.99888 65
                                           0.5         0.99888 65

2.6 Axial homogenization and Problems with the EU Plates
 Table 4 to Table 7 show the axial homogenization that was adopted in the cross section
processing of the drawer zones through BuildZPRModel. Note that separate cross sections are
generated for the core (Z1 and Z2), the axial DU blanket (Z3), the first axial steel reflector (Z4
is the last section of the front drawer), and the second axial steel reflector (Z5).
As mentioned in Section 2.1, the cladding on the HEU plates in ZPPR-15C and ZPPR-15D
loadings introduces two options for axial boundaries of 18.03 and 18.36 inches. Figure 7
provides a side by side comparison of a Pu loaded drawer (141 is the top one) and EU loaded
drawer (905 is the bottom one). The yellow colored plates are sodium, the light green are
depleted uranium, the purple is zirconium, deep red is Plutonium, and orange is enriched
uranium. As can be seen, the EU plate in 905 extends slightly into the blanket region.

 Figure 7. Schematic View of Drawer 141 and 905
 The preferred approach in DIF3D is to use a single zone for the core as dividing it up
into smaller pieces tends to expose the importance of slight differences in the plate sizes in the
collection of loaded drawers. A study was performed to determine what impact using a single
boundary of 18.03 versus 18.36 had on the problem. From Figure 7, when 18.36 is chosen, the
Pu loaded drawers end up having ~0.33 inches of blanket material smeared into them. This
causes a negative 300 pcm bias as the blanket material is smeared all the way to the center of the
core.

Conversely, choosing a single 18.03 inch boundary leads to ~0.33 inches of enriched uranium to
be smeared into the axial blanket region for all EU loaded drawers. This leads to a negative 200
pcm bias in the critical reference loading. Both of these decisions were deemed unwise and
choosing both 18.03 and 18.36 inch axial boundaries was best. Unfortunately, DIF3D-
VARIANT can have difficulty with small axial mesh sizes such as these (~0.84 cm). In DIF3D-
VARIANT, an axial mesh size less than ~0.9 cm should be avoided for fast spectrum and
divergence was observed to occur for the sodium void configurations of ZPPR-15D and thus
using 18.03 and 18.36 together is not feasible on all loadings.
 A analysis study on the sizing of this axial mesh was performed on ZPPR-15D Loading
185 and the results are presented in Table 8. In Table 8, the 18.03 inch border would seem to be
the ideal choice as it does not allow the EU to be smeared into the full blanket. However, because
the small axial section has a different plate arrangement, it is not clear how the cross sections
should be generated for this region. In Table 8, the results taking a homogeneous and
heterogeneous approach to cross section generation between 18.03 and 18.36 are provided. Other
alternatives would be to obtain the cross sections from the adjacent core region or adjacent
blanket region. For the heterogeneous case, it should be clear that the Pu loaded drawers default
to a homogeneous methodology as there is no enriched material present past 18.03 inches. For
the EU plates, the 1D heterogeneous model is built based upon the blanket region rather than the
active core. Choosing either approach has a rather negligible impact on the result and thus it is
reasonable to assume a homogeneous model for this transition region is likely sufficient.

 Axial Mesh
 keff Model Description
 Border (in)
              17.36        1.00608     Heterogeneous model between 17.36 and 18.36
              17.36        1.00606     Homogeneous model between 17.36 and 18.36
              17.56        1.00616     Heterogeneous model between 17.56 and 18.36
              17.76        1.00625     Homogeneous model between 17.76 and 18.36
              17.90        1.00632     Homogeneous model between 17.90 and 18.36
              18.03        1.00612     Homogeneous model between 18.03 and 18.36
              18.03        1.00611     Heterogeneous model between 18.03 and 18.36

 Continuing the analysis of the Table 8 results, four additional axial boundaries are
considered: 17.36, 17.56, 17.76, and 17.9 inches. All of these choices will impose some of the
core material is smeared into a small part of the blanket region (0.33 inches) in the Pu loaded
drawers. For the EU drawers, all of these choices will impose that a small amount of the axial
blanket is smeared in with the active core. As stated, only choosing the 18.03 inch boundary
ensures that no active core and blanket materials are artificially smeared. For the 17.36 inch
selection, results were obtained using a homogeneous and heterogeneous MC 2 model and the
impact on keff is almost negligible (2 pcm) between them, similar to that observed with the 18.03
inch results. Since both 17.36 inch models have identical DIF3D setups, the difference is purely
in the cross sections obtained from MC2-3 and the impact of those cross sections on the DIF3D
calculation. For the 17.56 inch selection, a heterogeneous model is used and one finds a slight
increase in keff (+8 pcm) compared with the 17.36 inch model. The 17.76 and 17.90 inch models

 19

both used homogeneous models as the 1D heterogeneous models are difficult to generate.
Looking at the trend of the smeared blanket/core models, k eff increases by ~30 pcm and this
would indicate that the 18.03 result should be ~640 pcm above critical. However, given the 18.03
inch model results are available, it should be clear that the smear model is simply inaccurate.
This is especially known to be true as the keff is observed to drop 200 to 300 pcm as the axial
mesh border is moved closer to the core center.
 Since 40 pcm is within the noise of the space-angle-energy approximations in DIF3D-
VARIANT, it does not materially matter which of the axial boundaries in Table 8 are chosen. In
that regard, the models for all ZPPR-15C and ZPPR-15D take the 17.36 inch boundary and use
a homogeneous model between 17.36 and 18.36. This last aspect turns out to be very important
because in some of the sodium void measurements (drawer 933 in particular), it becomes very
difficult to determine how to build a heterogeneous model to generate cross sections in this small
region and doing a homogenous model always works and leads to a consistent comparison
between the measurements based upon criticality.

 The Z1 and Z2 boundaries are only needed for some of the loadings that turn out to have
special features such as void zones or control rod materials. As an example, in ZPPR-15C
loadings 167, 168, and 169, the axial homogenization of the core was split at 8.03 inches to
accurately treat the voided zones in the stationary and movable drawers 154, 716, and 931 of
loading 168 and ensure consistency in the modeling between the three loadings. Figure 8 and
Figure 9 show example drawer diagrams with voided regions where additional drawer diagrams
can be found in Appendix B. The same 8.03 inch split was used for ZPPR-15D loading 189 for
the stationary and movable partially voided drawers 744 and 931.

 Figure 8. Schematic View of Drawer 931

 Figure 9. Schematic View of Drawer 933
3. Completed Analysis Work
 Most of the loadings listed in Table 1 were analyzed for the present V&V activity. For
each loading, the MC2+DIF3D and, when available, the MCNP results were compared with the
experimental measurement. Additionally, some commentary is provided on the suitability of
each case for fast reactor validation efforts.

 20

4. Analysis of Critical Cores
 Using the UC cross section methodology of BuildZPRModel, k eff values were calculated
with DIF3D-VARIANT for all four critical loadings of ZPPR-15. With each critical loading, a
detailed space-angle-energy refinement study was carried out to ascertain the best set of
approximations to use for all of the other loadings. In this section, the details of this study are
displayed and discussed. The keff values presented in Table 9 were obtained with DIF3D-
VARIANT with the following approximations:
  A P3 angular flux expansion and a P3 scattering kernel (P3P3).
  The standard 230 group energy structure [4].
  A 6th order x-y flux, 4th order z flux, 4th order source, and 1st order leakage spatial
 approximations (referred to here as F6-Z4-S4-L1 input).
  A maximum axial mesh size of 10 cm. (BuildZPRModel input allows the user to limit
 the maximum mesh size in X, Y, and Z and this limit was applied to all of the critical
 loadings.)
 Table 9. DIF3D-VARIANT Calculated keff for ZPPR-15 Critical Loadings
 DIF3D
 Loading
 keff
                                           ZPPR-15A L015       0.99999
                                           ZPPR-15B L088       0.99889
                                           ZPPR-15C L166       1.00372
                                           ZPPR-15D L185       1.00625

4.1 Impact of the Anisotropic Scattering Kernel

 Table 10 shows the impact on keff of using different anisotropic scattering kernels in
DIF3D-VARIANT. All of the calculations use a F6-Z4-S4-L1 spatial approximation combined
with a P5 angular approximation and 230 group energy structure. From the results, a P 3
anisotropic scattering kernel causes a ~50 pcm change with respect to a P 1 approximation.
However, no significant changes are observed when increasing to a P 5 order. For the 230 group
energy structure, this is the expected behavior as the energy band of each group is so large that
the true impact of anisotropic scattering is limited. With larger energy group structures, the order
of the anisotropic scattering kernel can be slightly more sensitive but, for neutrons, all past work
has indicated that P3 is sufficient up to ~1000 groups given the underlying uncertainty in the
cross section data itself.

4.2 Impact of the Angular Flux Expansion

 Table 11 shows the impact of the angular expansion order in VARIANT on k eff using a
fixed F6-Z4-S4-L1 spatial approximation, P1 scattering kernel, and 33 group energy structure. It
is important to note that 230 groups could not be used as the P 7 calculations all diverged. Further
the P5P1 to P3P1 comparison for 230 groups yielded 60-80 pcm which is considerably larger than
the 33 group results shown in Table 11. As discussed earlier, this is not unusual for DIF3D-
VARIANT which has trouble with small meshes and low density materials (empty matrix tube).
While the eigenvalue convergence is not identical between all four loadings, it should be clear
that angular refinements past P3 will yield less than 100 pcm of error.

4.3 Impact of the Spatial Approximations

 Table 12 shows the impact of the spatial source expansion on k eff. For all of these
calculations, either a F6-Z4-S4-L1 or F6-Z4-S6-L1 spatial approximation was used with a P 3
angular approximation, P 1 scattering kernel, and 230 group energy structure. As can be seen, the
spatial order of the source approximation is minor for this set of problems which is typical for
the mesh sizes used. It should be noted that the source approximation refinement has the opposite
direction impact on keff indicating some level of cancellation of error is occurring between the
space-angle approximations.

4.4 Impact of the Energy Group Structure
 Table 15 shows the impact of the energy group structure on k eff. For all of these
calculations a F6-Z4-S4-L1 spatial approximation was combined with a P 3 angular
approximation and P1 scattering kernel. The built-in group structures of ANL33, ANL70,
ANL116, ANL230 and ANL425 were chosen to generate these results.
 Table 15. Impact on Calculated keff due to the Energy Group Structure
                   230           33                     70                           116                      425
 Loading keff keff C1-R keff C2-R keff C3-R keff C4-R
                    (R)    (C1)     pcm         (C2)          pcm         (C3)             pcm      (C4)             pcm
      L015       0.99940 1.00011     71       1.00109          170      0.99954             14    0.99957             17
      L088       0.99842 0.99909      67      1.00007         165       0.99852             10    0.99867             25

Taking the 230 group as the targeted base dataset of the work, the table displays the differences
between the other energy group structures and the 230 group one. As can be seen, the error is
high when 33 and 70 groups are used, but the 116 and 425 group structures have significantly
smaller errors in line with the space-angle approximations shown above. There is of course no

 23

consistent convergence trend as the group structure is refined which is expected as the multi-
group cross section processing through equivalence theory will yield cancellation of error at all
energy group levels. In this regard, the only good guidance for selecting a particular energy group
structure is to choose one which is consistent with the most refined energy group structure
attempted. In this case one could have chosen the 116, 230, or 425 group structures for the base
calculations but 230 was chosen because of its wider recognition in the reactor analysis
community where 33 groups is used in typical analysis work today.

4.5 Impact of the Axial Mesh Size

 Table 16 shows the impact of the axial mesh size on keff where Table 17 shows the actual
meshing choices made by BuildZPRmodel. Refining the radial meshing is not possible as
DIF3D-VARIANT transport calculations will not converge for such small mesh sizes. For all of
these calculations a F6-Z4-S4-L1 spatial approximation was combined with a P 3 angular
approximation, P1 scattering kernel, and 230 group cross section library. From the results, it
should be clear that refining the axial mesh yields almost no improvement. Coarsening the mesh
yields relatively minor changes in keff.

 24

 To understand the observed behavior, DIF3D-VARIANT deploys a p-refinement
strategy such that any additional, unnecessary interfaces (i.e. those that do not correspond to
material boundaries), will only alter the spatial polynomial order needed to obtain spatial
convergence. In this regard, as the mesh is refined, a lower order leakage approximation can be
used to couple the nodes together and thus a lower order spatial flux expansion. Conversely, as
the mesh size is coarsened, a higher order leakage approximation is required to obtain the same
accuracy as that used in the finer mesh cases. Because the linear order leakage is the bare
minimum required to model the spatial dependence, it should come as no surprise that mesh
refinement beyond 10 cm has no impact (BuildZPRmodel has a input limit of 8 cm to prevent
the user from building unwise inputs). One should be able to see a consistent behavior between
the results of Table 16 and that of Table 14.

4.6 Adopted Approximation Orders for Reference Calculations

 Given the preceding results, the bulk of the DIF3D-VARIANT calculations of the ZPPR-
15 loadings use the following input settings:
  A P3 angular flux expansion
  A P3 scattering kernel
  A 230 group energy structure
  A F6-Z4-S4-L1 (040621) spatial approximation
  An axial mesh size of 10 cm
All of the models are built using the UC option of BuildZPRModel with ENDF\B-VII.0 cross
section data. All Monte Carlo calculations presented in this document were obtained with
MCNP6.1 and ENDF\B-VII.0 nuclear data based on the “as built” models that BuildZPRModel
takes as its input.
 Table 18 provides the DIF3D-VARIANT and Monte Carlo k eff predictions for each
critical loading of ZPPR-15 along with the experimentally measured values. Each Monte Carlo
calculation was converged to a 0.00002 one-sigma deviation on k eff. Note that each loading was
slightly supercritical and that the measured value includes bias adjustments and an uncertainty
that includes the measurement error and the loading uncertainties. From Table 18, one can see a
relatively consistent result between DIF3D and MCNP with respect to the experimentally
measured value. While DIF3D-VARIANT is generally less accurate, the 100-200 pcm error in
keff for a deterministic code is more than acceptable for predictive analysis work.

5. Analysis of ZPPR-15 Simulated Control Rod Measurements
 The ZPPR-15 measurement of control rod worth [22] used between 1 and 13 mockup
control rods (CRs) or control rod positions (CRPs) an example of which is shown in Figure 10.
CR/CRPs are not to be confused with the operational control rods used in ZPPR that consist of
narrow stainless steel-clad blades of boron carbide inside stainless steel guide tubes and are
referred to as poison safety rods or poison shim rods (PSRs).

 Figure 10. ZPPR-15B Loading Showing Simulated Control Rod Positions (622)

A CRP consisted of a 2 x 2 array of drawers filled with sodium plates over the entire 36-in drawer
length. In general, a CR consisted of a 2 x 2 array of drawers with 50% B 4C plates and 50%
sodium plates in the front 18-in (core zone) of the drawers. The back 18-in of a CR drawer were

 26

filled with sodium plates. In a few experiments, the front 18-in of a CR drawer were completely
filled with B4C plates.
 As seen in Figure 10, the 13 CR/CRP positions are arranged in three rings. Ring 1 referred
to just the central position in the core (CR 1), while Ring 2 refers to a middle ring containing six
CR/CRP positions (CRs 2 – 7). Ring 3 refers to an outer ring containing six CR/CRP positions
(CRs 8 – 13). Rings 1 and 3 (CRs 1, 8 – 13) were designated as primary CRs, and Ring 2 (CRs
2 – 7) was designated as secondary CRs. In ZPPR-15D loading 209 (L209), there was a
segmented CR having 100% B4C plates in the 0 – 4-in and 10 – 18-in positions. The remainder
of the drawer was filled with columns of stainless steel plates and sodium plates.
 Table 19 provides a summary description on the type, number, and positions of the
CR/CRPs in all ZPPR-15 loadings that provide control rod worth measurements. The drawer
designation for a typical fast reactor control rod was 622 for a B 4C filled (or rodded) position
with sodium. Drawer type 601 was the CRP meant to be the empty control rod position while
drawer type 602 was filled with all B4C plates and type 603 was 50% B4C and 50% sodium. In
most cases both halves of the matrix were filled with the control rod but in several cases one half
is rodded while the other has the empty control rod position model. Drawer type 628 is a special
segmented control rod while 629 is a fully natural B 4C plate filled drawer similar to 602.

 Table 20, Table 21, and Table 22 provide the DIF3D and MCNP calculated k eff values
along with the with the measurement criticality results for the control rod loadings of ZPPR-
15A, ZPPR-15B, and ZPPR-15D, respectively. The experimental results were adjusted for room
return, DU impurities, Kel-F coating on the DU plates, HEU impurities and corrosion in the clad
HEU plates (leaking end caps) as they are neglected in the as-built models and thus DIF3D and
MCNP calculations. The errors for the experimental results only consider the measurement
uncertainty and do not contain the geometry and material uncertainties like those provided earlier
for the critical loadings. From a quick survey of the results, one can observe that for near critical

 27

loadings, both DIF3D and MCNP obtain results that are very consistent with the reported
experimental measurements and similar to the <200 pcm results observed earlier for the critical
reference loadings. However, for those loadings that are considerably sub-critical, i.e. loading
102 of ZPPR-15B and loading 199 of ZPPR-15D, both MCNP and DIF3D have substantial
errors. The third column in each table shows that the bias between the MCNP and DIF3D
calculations is not constant over the set of control rod worth measurements indicating that the
worth calculation itself will exhibit the same consistency problem. It is noted that the bias
between MCNP and DIF3D increases as the system becomes more sub-critical.

 For ZPPR-15D, the results for loadings 194 to 199 were reported relative to 193, while
loadings 209 and 210 were reported relative to 208. From the results, none of the differences

 29

between the calculated and experimental control rod worths are statistically significant. The
DIF3D and MCNP results also look quite consistent. While the ideal situation for all of the
control rod worth calculations would be zero computed error, the preceding calculations are all
considered very good given the uncertainty in the measured control rod worths and the
underlying errors in the cross-section data itself. It is important to note that most of the
uncertainty in the measured control rod worth results from the 5% uncertainty in β eff which is
required to convert from the measurement units, cents, to the calculation units, pcm, and vice
versa.

6. Analysis of Void Worth
 Sodium voiding is a key concern in a sodium cooled fast spectrum reactor and thus was
measured in all phases of ZPPR-15 [22]. The sodium cans were sealed, and the sodium in the
cans was solid. It was not possible to change the density of the sodium in the cans, so it was not
possible to perform experiments with reduced sodium density. Consequently, sodium void
experiments in ZPPR-15 consisted of complete voiding of sodium in the void region. A sodium-
voided zone was simulated in ZPPR by replacing sodium-filled stainless steel cans, i.e., sodium
plates, with empty stainless steel cans (void cans) in the drawers in a selected region of the core.
 For ZPPR-15A, the void region consisted of the central 148 drawers in each half. In
phases B, C and D, the void region consisted of the central 52 drawers in each half. Axial void
regions of 8-in, 14-in and 18-in in each half were simulated in all phases. Phase A also simulated
a 31-in void in each half (voiding of the 18-in core and the 13-in axial blanket in each half). In
addition, sodium voiding was simulated in a special high zirconium zone constructed during the
transition from ZPPR-15A to ZPPR-15B and in twelve blanket drawers in each half at the core-
blanket interface in ZPPR-15B.
 As was the case for the control rod worth, the modified source multiplication technique
was used to measure the sodium void worth. All PSRs were fully withdrawn for the sodium void
worth measurements and for the subcritical reference configuration. The published uncertainty
for each sodium void worth measurement is the statistical uncertainty for the measurement plus
the small uncertainties related to adjustment of the measured value to the temperature, Pu-241
decay date and interface gap of the relevant subcritical reference. Nearly all of the geometrical
and composition uncertainties are fully correlated between the subcritical reference
configuration and the sodium void configurations, so those uncertainties drop out of the
uncertainty analysis.
 Several additional uncertainties must be included for analysis of sodium void worth
measurements. Small contributions arise from uncertainty in the average core temperature and
from uncertainties in the masses and compositions of sodium plates removed to create the void
region. There is also a very small contribution from the effect of plate composition uncertainties
on calculated uncertainties. The reproducibility uncertainty is negligible for control rod worths
because the control rod worths are much larger than the associated reproducibility uncertainty.
That is not necessarily true for sodium void worths. As the total worth becomes small, which is
common for sodium void worths, reproducibility uncertainty becomes more significant. A
second aspect of reproducibility uncertainty to consider is that there is more potential for plate
shifting and uncertainty related to the exact positions of plates in the drawers of a sodium void

 30

measurement than in a control rod measurement. The reproducibility uncertainty is included in
the total measurement uncertainty.
 The worth per kilogram for stainless steel is much larger than the worth per kilogram for
sodium, so any mismatch between the mass of stainless steel removed in the sodium cans and
the mass of steel added in the void cans can introduce a significant experimental uncertainty. To
minimize this experimental uncertainty, the experimenters attempted to match the amount of
steel in the sodium plate to be removed with the amount of steel in the void can which replaced
that sodium plate. In practice, matching the stainless steel mass meant that each sodium plate
was replaced by a void can with the same dimensions. For example, a 1/2x2x8-in sodium plate
was replaced by a 1/2x2x8-in void can. This is clear from the drawer master figures or templates
used by the ZPPR technicians as guides to load plates into drawers. Note that only the sodium
plates were replaced and that no other plate in the affected drawers was moved or replaced in
order to minimize experimental uncertainty.
 Changing the core loading required shutting down the reactor, separating the matrix
halves, making the desired changes in the core loading, bringing the matrix halves together again
and moving the operational control rods to establish the desired configuration. There was some
Pu-241 decay during the elapsed time, and the average reactor temperature could change slightly
as a result of the time required to establish the new configuration. Also, there could be a small
difference in the interface gap between the reference configuration and the modified
configuration. Measuring the sodium void worth for multiple configurations typically required
several days. In order to minimize experimental uncertainty and facilitate analysis, the
experimenters adjusted the measured values for sodium void worth to a common decay date,
temperature and interface gap using measured Pu-241 decay, temperature, and interface gap
coefficients. These coefficients were measured during the operational measurements performed
after the reference critical configuration was established.
 The dominant contribution to the total uncertainty for most sodium void worth
measurements is the assumed 5% uncertainty in β eff [22]. The total uncertainty for each sodium
void worth configuration is slightly greater than 5% due to contributions from other
uncertainties, noting that the βeff uncertainty still dominates the actual error.

6.1 ZPPR-15A Sodium Void Worth Measurements
 The initial sub-critical reference for the ZPPR-15A sodium void worth measurements
was loading 16. An 8 inch (20.32 cm) void zone was established in 148 drawers per half in
loading 17. Because the measured void worth was large and positive in loading 17, eight double
fuel column (DCF) drawers were converted to single fuel column (SCF) drawers to create a
second sub-critical reference in loading 18. Loading 18 had an 8 inch (20.32 cm) void in 148
drawers per half because the only change from loading 17 to 18 was the conversion of eight DCF
drawers in loading 17 to eight SCF drawers in loading 18. The ZPPR staff resorted to converting
DCF drawers to SCF drawers to reduce reactivity which was referred to as “despike” the reactor.
Conversely, converting SCF drawers to DCF drawers to increase reactivity was referred to as a
“spike” to the loading.
        In loadings 19, 20 and 21, the void zones were 14 in (35.56 cm), 18 in (45.72 cm) and 31
in (78.74 cm), respectively, in 148 drawers per half. In loading 22, all void cans in the void zone

 31

in half 2 were replaced by sodium cans. In loading 23, all void cans in the void zone in half 1
were replaced by sodium cans to return to a sub-critical flooded reference configuration.
 In the ZPPR-15A sodium void worth measurements, detector drawers within the 148
drawer voiding zone were not voided, and the normal core drawers opposite those detector
drawers in the matrix were not voided. In ZPPR-15B, ZPPR-15C and ZPPR-15D, all drawers
within the voiding zone including any detector drawers were voided.

6.2 ZPPR-15B Sodium Void Worth Measurements
 The reference critical configuration for ZPPR-15B was loading 88, and the sub-critical
reference configuration was loading 89. It was necessary to reduce the core reactivity further for
the sodium void worth measurements. This was again accomplished, in loading 91, by a
“despike.” Thus, the sub-critical reference configuration for sodium void worth measurements
in ZPPR-15B was loading 91.
 In loading 92, an 8-in (20.32 cm) sodium void was introduced in the central 52 drawers
per half. In loading 93, the sodium void zone was increased to 14-in (35.56 cm) in the central 52
drawers per half. For loading 94, the sodium void zone was increased to 18-in (45.72 cm) in the
central 52 drawers per half. In loading 95, all sodium void cans in the void zone were replaced
by sodium plates to reconstruct loading 91. The reported 0.13¢ (0.437 pcm) difference between
the measured excess reactivities of loading 91 and 95 represents the reproducibility uncertainty
of the sodium void worth measurements.

6.3 ZPPR-15B High Zr Zone Sodium Void Worth Measurements
 During the transition from ZPPR-15A to ZPPR-15B, a high zirconium zone was
constructed by replacing two 1/16-in columns of depleted uranium plus two 1/16-in columns of
stainless steel with two 1/8-in columns of zirconium in a central zone of 132 drawers per half. A
subcritical reference was established for the high zirconium zone core in loading 76. In loading
77, an 8-in (20.32 cm) void zone was established in 52 drawers per half, and the worth of sodium
voiding was measured. Loading 78 was a reconstruction of loading 76 and the resulting 0.47¢
difference (1.580 pcm) is the reproducibility uncertainty.

6.4 ZPPR-15B Sodium Manometer Experiments
 In ZPPR-15B loadings 109 to 113, the worth of a simulated sodium manometer was
measured where loading 109 is the sub-critical reference for this set of measurements. A sodium
manometer is a tube containing gas that is trapped between a sealed end at the top and liquid
sodium below. Under normal operating conditions the gas is compressed at the top, and the liquid
sodium nearly fills the tube. The pressure drop in a loss-of-flow accident causes the sodium to
be expelled from the tube which reduces reactivity.
 In loading 110, the manometer was simulated by replacing twelve blanket drawers in
each half by drawers filled with sodium plates. In loading 111, the sodium plates in the
manometer drawers were replaced with void cans in half 1. In loading 112 the sodium plates in
the manometer drawers in half 2 were replaced with void cans. Loading 113 was a rebuild of the
sub-critical reference and thus the 0.47¢ difference (1.580 pcm) between the excess reactivities
is the reproducibility uncertainty.

 32

6.5 ZPPR-15C Sodium Void Worth Measurements
 The sub-critical reference configuration for ZPPR-15C was loading 165. As was the case
for the previous two phases, it was necessary to reduce the core excess reactivity further for the
sodium void worth measurements. Thus, selected DCF drawers in the outer core were converted
to SCF drawers to produce the sodium void worth reference configuration of loading 167
(despiked). In loading 168, an 8-in (20.32 cm) sodium void zone was introduced in the central
52 drawers per half. In loading 169, the sodium void zone was increased to 18-in (45.72 cm) in
the central 52 drawers per half. All of the void cans in the void zone were replaced by sodium
plates in loading 170 to reproduce loading 167. The 0.15¢ difference (0.781 pcm) between the
excess reactivities of loading 167 and 170 represents the reproducibility uncertainty on ZPPR-
15C.

6.6 ZPPR-15D Sodium Void Worth Measurements
 The subcritical sodium void worth configuration for ZPPR-15D was loading 184. In
loading 189, an 8-in (20.32 cm) sodium void was introduced in the central 52 drawers per half.
In loading 190, the sodium void zone was increased to 18 in (45.72 cm) in the central 52 drawers
per half. All void cans in the void zone were replaced by sodium plates in loading 191 which is
the reproduction of loading 184. The 0.13¢ (0.853 pcm) difference between the excess
reactivities of loading 184 and 191 represents the reproducibility uncertainty.

6.7 Comparison of Measured and Calculated Sodium Void Worths in ZPPR-15.

 Table 26 to Table 31 list the keff results for the individual loadings and estimated
experimental measurement errors derived from the reported worths. For direct comparison, the
calculated values obtained with DIF3D and MCNP are presented in Table 26 to Table 31. The
experimental result of keff for Loading 17 is considered to be unreliable and thus only a
comparison between MCNP and DIF3D is possible on that loading. All of the remaining
experimental keff values were adjusted for room return, impurities in the DU, and the Kel-F
coating on the DU plates as they were neglected in the as-built models. All of the MCNP
calculations were converged to a statistical standard deviation of 0.00002.
 For MCNP calculations, the as-built models contain minimal approximations and thus
the reported worth results can be computed directly. For DIF3D calculations, the approximations
made in the MC2-3 lattice calculation can impact the solution significantly as discussed earlier.
This action can thereby add error to the calculation much like using a 33 group approximation
for one loading and a 230 group approximation in another and computing the worth between the
calculated results. As a consequence, it is important to impose the same lattice assumptions in
MC2 between two loadings that constitute the measured worth. Unfortunately, the reported
worths are between consecutive loadings or amongst loadings that were part of a sequence. At a
minimum, the deterministic approach would require at least two models for each loading in the
sequence which is cumbersome. As an alternative, the reported void worths were recomputed to
all be relative to the reference sub-critical loading for the sequence. As an example, the void
worth for L023 is reported as the difference in worth computed between loadings 22 and 23. For
the deterministic code, the approach taken is to compute the worth between loadings 16 and 22,
and then loadings 16 and 23. When combined, the worth between loading 22 and 23 can be
computed and thus the experimental measurement reproduced. This approach works because

 33

loading 16, the reference sub-critical loading for the sequence, can be manipulated to provide
the same MC2 approximation for all later loadings, such as loadings 22 and 23.
 In each table, there are multiple keff values provided by DIF3D for the sub-critical loading
where the notation Lx/Ly indicates that the modeling of loading Lx=L016 has been manipulated
to match the MC2 input assumptions required for loading Ly=L017. As can be seen, taking this
approach minimizes the number of additional input models required to be constructed from the
expected two per loading. One can also observe that each change in the modeling assumptions
of the sub-critical reference causes significant changes in the predicted k eff on the order of 100
pcm from the unperturbed state in the cases demonstrated. What is key of course is that the bias
in the predicted criticality for the two loadings are consistent. In this regard one should note that
there is very little consistency in the bias for DIF3D on many of the loadings with variations
between 5 and 250 pcm. MCNP on the other hand shows a rather consistent bias between all
loadings with common features and thus one would expect those results to be more reliable.

 A quick survey of Table 26 to Table 31 indicates that most of the sodium void worth
measurements involve a loading which is very near critical (typically 200 pcm below critical and
at most 600 pcm). Thus, unlike the preceding control rod worth measurements, the total bias
does not become larger than that typical with the critical loading results shown earlier. Of all of
the tables, the ZPPR-15D results in Table 31 are particularly inconsistent with the other tables
and the previous critical reference configurations.

 Table 32 to Table 35 provide the reactivity worth Δρ comparison obtained as:
 Δρ = ρperturbed – ρreference,
where ρperturbed is the reactivity of the perturbed state (by sodium void) and ρreference is the
reactivity of the reference state. Table 32 to Table 35 provide the calculated and measured
sodium void worths. The first column indicates the pair of loadings for which the reactivity
difference was measured. The largest errors appear with the smallest void worths. The
reproducibility error assessed above is well below the magnitude of error observed in these small
worth measurements. Some of the more concerning issues are the large increases in computed
error in DIF3D relative to MCNP. For DIF3D, good results are only achieved for the larger
sodium void experiments in the ZPPR-15B series (excluding the manometer experiments) and
only for the first two of the ZPPR-15A series. The MCNP results also have large errors for many
of the smaller void worth measurements (< 30 pcm) which make the use of those experiments
for validation purposes questionable.
 One explanation for the higher errors at lower reactivity worths is the imperfect nature
of the loading details. As an example, loading 190 of ZPPR-15D has a reported -4 pcm worth. It
is unrealistic to expect that differences in the interface gap, reactor temperature distribution, and
reproducibility do not impact the two experiments sufficiently to perturb this measurement.
There are several other factors that can affect the sodium void worth measurement, the associated
experimental uncertainty and the models. First, the as-built models are based on the average
sodium plate and void can of each size used in the experiment. In reality, there were small plate-
to-plate variations between plates of the same size due to normal manufacturing tolerances. The
plate-to-plate variations and the actual position of each plate have a small effect on measured
reactivity. Second, although experimental procedures were designed to minimize plate shifting,
i.e., changes in plate positions due to normal drawer loading/unloading operations and drawer
handling, plate shifting could still occur. Small shifts in plate positions in the drawers affected
measured reactivity. Third, there is an uncertainty in control rod calibration that affects the
measured values. Fourth, there is a small uncertainty in the position of each control rod. Fifth,
the interface gap was not a constant value. It could be affected by the weight of the loaded
drawers. The net effect of these factors is small. However, the relative importance of this net
effect and its relative contribution to both the total experimental uncertainty and the difference

 36

between measured and calculated worths would be greater for a small measured value than for a
large measured value.
 In several of the other low reactivity worth results, both MCNP and DIF3D do rather well
(loading 110 to 112) given such issues. In this regard, several of the reactivity worth
measurements are not suggested to be used in the validation effort as they can be considered
unreliable. These are highlighted in each table primarily based upon the MCNP results as the
DIF3D calculation should be consistent with MCNP.

7. Analysis of Neutron Spectrum Measurements
 Neutron spectra were measured near the center of the core in ZPPR-15A loading 66,
ZPPR-15B loading 90 and ZPPR-15D loading 186. Neutron spectra also were measured at the
center of the simulated shield as part of the ZPPR-15B shield experiment, specifically in ZPPR-
15B loading 121 (SSNA configuration) and ZPPR-15B loading 126 (BCNA configuration). In
the initial shield configuration, there was a large simulated stainless steel/sodium pool, i.e., a
region whose drawers contained primarily stainless steel plates and sodium plates, in the shield
zone. This configuration was designated SSNA by the experimenters. Some of the stainless steel
plates in the stainless steel/sodium pool were subsequently replaced with boron carbide plates to
form the BCNA shield configuration. The purpose of the special shielding region was to provide
information on flux attenuation in the simulated sodium pool.
 The neutron spectrum measurements in loadings 121 and 126 provide additional
validation data for shielding analysis in the reactor system. The eigenvalue results for these
loadings are consistent with the ZPPR-15B critical result shown earlier and are omitted here for
brevity.

7.1 Measured and Calculated Neutron Spectra
 The neutron spectra were measured with hydrogen and methane proportional counters in
proton recoil spectrometers and both were only used in Loading 66, 90, and 186 but only the
methane counter was used in loadings 121 and 126. These counters could measure spectra in the
1.2 keV – 2.2 MeV energy range. In the Monte Carlo “as built” models, the flux tallies are
obtained within the volume of the methane counter that is modeled as accurately as can be.. A
fifteen energy group structure between 1.2 keV – 2.2 MeV was used for the tallies to match the
energy group structure reported by the experimenters. The energy boundaries of these fifteen
groups matched energy group boundaries in the standard 21 group cross section libraries used
for reactor analysis at Argonne when ZPPR-15 was built. In the deterministic models, the counter
cannot be accurately represented and the fluxes are evaluated at several places in the volume of
the detector itself as shown in Figure 11 for ZPPR-15A loading 66. It should be noted that the
MCNP model does have the lead shield present around the detector but the color is very faint
while the DIF3D model has a more clearer color but geometrically skewed representation due to
the plotting software.

 Figure 11. Model Measurement Details for the Neutron Spectrum Measurement
 For the deterministic calculations, the DIF3D flux spectra was obtained using the 230
energy group structure and was collapsed to the 21 group energy structure of the reported
experimental values. While the DIF3D models include the lead shield and steel ring around the

 38

detectors, the methodology requires these materials get smeared with the matrix tube and drawer
compositions. For those loadings with two different detectors, the only difference is the control
rod locations and the DIF3D models were generated and performed for each. The spatial variance
results are given for the hydrogen detector along with a common evaluation point for the methane
detector configuration. To obtain the spectrum, the EvaluateFlux utility was used to pick points
within the location of the detector and additional points in adjacent drawers to give a sense of
the importance of the spatial selection point on the spectrum.
 Starting with the neutron spectra measurements in Loading 66, Figure 12 displays the
DIF3D and MCNP predictions agains the reported flux measurement results where the DIF3D
and MCNP result were both normalized to the same area as the measurement result. The MCNP
result used 1 billion neutron histories and had rather low statistical errors. The DIF3D result is
for a point evaluated at the exact center of the detector. Because the measurement did not cover
the entire energy range of the typical MCNP or DIF3D model (1.2 keV to 2.2 MeV instead of 0
eV to 20 MeV), the MCNP result for the upper energy range was not broken up and thus is not
plotted. As can be seen, the MCNP and DIF3D results are rather similar to each other with
notable differences. The Loading 66 control rod differences made minor differences in the
evaluated spectrum result in DIF3D which was expected. Both MCNP and DIF3D have non-
trivial differences from the measured result and for this case the comparison is displayed in Table
36.

 Figure 12. ZPPR-15A L066 Normalized Neutron Spectra
As can be seen in Table 36, the maximum error on high confidence measurement results is ~10%.
While the reported energy range measurement values calculated by DIF3D and MCNP are well
outside of 3- σ, it is equally likely that the measurement error is underestimated and that the
DIF3D and MCNP results are biased due to errors in the cross section data or the model itself.

 39

Regardless, those measurement errors with low confidence do present a problem as they can alter
the overall normalization and thus comparison itself.

 All of the neutron spectrum results that will be shown in the section have the same basic
behavior to that seen in Table 36 and thus no additional tables of comparitive results are given.
To give a sense of the importance of the measurement position, Figure 13 provides the error in
the flux distribution between different spatial evaluation points in the domain. The first set of
points only comparisons in the axial point selection within the detector volume while the second
set compares spectrum changes when the point is selected in adjacent drawers in the x-direction
of the model. The x and z coordinates are specific to the DIF3D model and should not be
compared against the original coordinate system of the machine. Looking at the results in Figure
13, one can see that the axial evaluation point in the detector (z=98.2 cm to z=91.1 cm) yields a
solution that is within 1% error of the evaluation point at the center of the detector (x=157.4 cm
and z=94.6 cm). Evaluating the spectrum in the adjacent drawers has a harder spectrum which
is consistent with the detector position not containing plutonium fuel while the adjacent drawers
do. Even with this aspect, the differences are less than 5% within the range of importance for the
measurement being less than 3% difference.

results are similar but both have errors relative to the measurement. For the measurements with
higher confidence, the errors in the MCNP and DIF3D solutions are again a maximum of ~12%
and appear in the same energy groups as those seen for L066. The difference in spatial evaluation
point show a near identical behavior to L066 and are not included here for brevity.
