 42

 Looking at Figure 12 through Figure 15, the MCNP and DIF3D results are consistent
with rather small errors. Non-negligible discrepancies are observable between the calculated and
experimental neutron spectra, especially in the part of the spectrum with the most neutrons.
However, the uncertainties published by the experimenters are the uncertainties due to counting
statistics. The experimenters estimated additional uncertainties below 10 keV and above 1 MeV
along with the correlation related issues. With the change in distribution of error in the calculated
spectrum results between Figure 12 and Figure 15, it should be clear that the cross section data
or the models in MCNP and DIF3D are the most likely source of the errors as the measurement
equipment itself would not degrade with a switch in fuel content. The errors are most likely a
combined problem with the normalization, an underestimate of the measurement uncertainty,
and a fundamental problem with the cross section data over the entire energy regime. Overall,
the calculated results are acceptable for all three loadings for the intended use. It should be clear
that DIF3D and MCNP will overestimate the fast neutron fluxes (> 100 keV) with the current
cross section library.
 Unlike the preceding spectrum measurements, the spectrum measurements in the special
shielding zone of ZPPR-15B (loading 121 and 126) are particularly difficult to obtain with
MCNP. Even with one billion histories, non-negligible statistical errors were obtained for both
experiments. Figure 16 shows the comparison for loading 121 while Figure 17 shows the
comparison for loading 126. The flux is considerably thermalized in both measurements
compared with the core center measurements. Loading 121 has a considerably larger thermal
peak than 126 due to the presence of boron in the latter. Very large statistical errors are observed
in the MCNP results, especially in loading 126 due to the flux depression caused by the
surrounding boron. For the experimental measurements, the difference in position impacts the
irradiation time required to achieve acceptable counting statistics. In order to obtain comparable
statistics to the core center measurements, the number of histories in the MCNP calculation
would have to be increased by a factor of ~100 which is not practical with the existing computing
resources.
 Table 38 provides the tabulated values of the comparison for loading 121 where only a
methane detector was used for the measurement and the 1-sigma errors for the MCNP results are
given because they are non-trivial. Despite the difficultly encountered with MCNP, the results
are only terrible for those parts of the spectrum where MCNP has difficulty getting an answer.
The DIF3D results for the high confidence part of the measurement are less than 15% and it is
likely that the differences between DIF3D and MCNP would lessen if more particles were
possible in MCNP. Table 39 provides the tabulated data for loading 126 which shows a notable
improvement in the comparison between DIF3D and the measurement for the high confidence
measurements. It is worth noting that the high error results of DIF3D are not too far outside of
the 3-σ errors on the measurement because the measurement errors are so large. The MCNP
result for loading 126 can be disregarded because of the high statistical errors. These
measurements highlight the difficulties that predictive analysis software have in obtaining
accurate flux and cross section data in regions far from the reactor core. It is not clear what value
these last two measurements are at this time, but it does indicate that existing MCNP and DIF3D
predictions in the ex-core regions can contain significant errors.

 The last aspect to deal with is the impact of the spatial evaluation point in the DIF3D
model. The preceding results in loading 121 and 126 were for the centrally located point in the

 45

detector position. Figure 18 shows the impact of choosing different evaluation points. The first
six points are different than the centrally located point (x=41.4 cm and z=94.6 cm) while the last
four points are for selecting points in the adjacent drawer compared against the centrally located
point. As can be seen, the errors for the points in the detector volume yield very small deviations
from the centrally located point. Choosing a point in the adjacent drawer has a much larger
impact which would have considerable changes when compared against the measurement. It
should be clear from the plots that the spectrum gets harder as the evaluation point moves toward
the core (x > 41.4 cm) and more thermal as it moves away from the core (x < 41.4 cm). Because
the flux solution does vary smoothly within the mesh, it is very likely the DIF3D result can be
manipulated by varying the evaluation point within the drawer in the x-direction. Whether this
would produce a better or worse comparison against the experiment is not clear and the current
centrally located position is the most logical choice for this work and thus no further effort was
taken.

 Figure 18. ZPPR-15B L126 Impact of Spatial Evaluation Point
8. Measured and Calculated B-10(n,α) Reaction Rates
 The B-10(n,α) reaction rate was measured as part of the ZPPR-15B shield experiment.
The measurements were performed by placing a flowing gas proportional counter into a drawer
at six locations in the shield, radial reflector, blanket, and core of the ZPPR-15B BCNA shielding
experiments (see Section 7) for a summary description of the BCNA configuration. The counter
was a cylindrical back-to-back (BTB) counter divided into two chambers. One chamber
contained a rectangular deposit of enriched boron (96.5% B-10) on a stainless steel backing. The
boron deposit was sufficiently thin such that the boron could be regarded as infinitely dilute for
analysis purposes. The other chamber was empty. Counts were recorded in both chambers of the
BTB counter. Counts in the empty chamber were subtracted from the total number of counts in

 46

the boron chamber to correct for gamma-induced counts in the boron chamber. There was only
one B-10 BTB proportional counter. Thus, the B-10(n,α) reaction rate was measured in the
ZPPR-15B shield experiment by moving this counter from one matrix location to another in six
successive loadings (L127 to L132). Figure 19 shows the core loading and counter positions in
the ZPPR matrix for these measurements. Table 41 and Table 40 show the correspondence
between the loading number and counter position. The B-10 proportional counter was located in
half 1 and the matrix loading in half 2 was not changed during these measurements. Similar to
the neutron spectrum measurement, the DIF3D and MCNP bias for each loading was consistent
with that observed with the critical reference loading of ZPPR-15B.

 Figure 19. Matrix Locations for the B-10(n,α) Reaction Rate Measurements
 The exact boron mass in the BTB counter deposit and the efficiency of the counter are
unknown, so it is not possible to determine absolute reaction rates. The experimenters
normalized the reported values to 10.0 in matrix location 158-35, and the calculated results were
normalized in the same way. In the MCNP calculation, the BTB counter volume was explicitly
modeled and results were obtained with approximately one billion histories via the FM4 tally
multiplier with reaction number 107 (i.e., n,α reaction) for the B-10 isotope. Table 40 shows the

 47

corresponding comparison for the MCNP calculations where the 1-sigma errors are observed to
increase substantially in loadings 128 and 127.

For DIF3D, the flux solution can be evaluated at different points in the detector and B-10 detector
cross sections need to be generated for each location. Two evaluation points at 12.7 and 20 cm
from the matrix interface were chosen to be the assumed position of the B-10 detector plate.
Only the results for the 12.7 cm position are displayed as the 20 cm result yielded a result nearly
identical to the 12.7 cm one.
 The drawers replaced by insertion of the measurement device are 105, 413, 415, 414, 410
and a investigation of the cross section changes was done on drawer type 105. Figure 20 shows
the difference in the plate loading of drawer 105 and 815. As can be seen, the central section in
drawer type 815 is not present as that is where the detector is loaded.

 Figure 20. Loading Difference Between Drawer 105 (upper) and Drawer 815 (lower)
For the MC2 model, the correct approach to generating the cross sections for the DIF3D model
involves building a 1D slab geometry representation of the drawer. For drawer 815, this approach
is inappropriate and it should be clear that the volume fraction of the various plates will be
different in this region. For foil measurements, the placement of the foils in the plate geometry
is very important to getting a correct solution. To get B-10 detector cross sections, three
approaches were taken which add a small amount of B-10 to 1) the matrix tube, 2) the steel
cladding of the plutonium plate, and 3) every plate in the 1D model. Figure 21 shows the impact
this has on the B-10 detector cross sections where the base detector cross section was obtained
by putting the B-10 in a homogeneous mixture of drawer type 105.

 48

 Figure 21. B-10 Detector Cross Section Changes for Drawer 105
As can be seen, the B-10 detector cross sections when this approach is taken have errors in the
20-30% range. Because this point was chosen as the normalization point, the decisions made
here can impact the fit of all of the points.
 For all other drawers, the MC2 approach to generating cross sections for DIF3D involves
simple homogeneous models and because drawers 413, 415, 414, and 410 do not contain
uranium or plutonium, a simple fission spectrum source will be assumed by MC 2. Figure 22
shows the B-10 cross sections evaluated by adding a small amount of B-10 to the fully
homogenized section of each drawer type in the active core. Drawer 501 is the blanket drawer
and the B-10 cross sections are not needed for this analysis work, but are provided for
comparison. The reference result is the B-10 cross section obtained from drawer 105 when a
slight amount of B-10 is added to the drawer. As can be seen, the differences in the B-10 detector
cross sections for all other drawer types are very small compared with the drawer 105 result. The
results shown are for the case where the leakage spectrum fixed source option of MC 2 is used as
opposed to an assumed fission spectrum source.
 The last aspect to consider for all of this is the cross section contribution to the reaction
rate. While the cross section differences might seem large in Figure 21, their contribution to the
overall reaction rate might not be that important. Figure 23 shows the reaction rate contribution
by energy group for each of the six measurement positions of the B-10 counter.

 49

Figure 22. B-10 Detector Cross Section Differences for Different Drawers

Figure 23. B-10 Reaction Rate Results for Different Measurement Positions

 50

As can be seen, except for loading 131, the majority of the reaction rate contribution is coming
from the 1 keV to 1 MeV range and thus the differences in the cross section shown earlier have
a rather minor impact on the final reaction rate calculation. For completeness, Figure 24 shows
the normalized spectrum results in each measurement position of the six loadings. As can be
seen, the spectrum for a measurement position in the active core (loading 132), is considerably
harder than the other 5 measurement positions and has very low levels of low energy neutrons.
Loadings 128 to 130 all reside in the heavy borated region surrounding the core and one can see
a significant drop in the low energy neutrons due to the absorption in boron. Similarly, the
simulated sodium pool region, loading 127, has a depressed thermal tail although not as
negligible as the other regions. In the modified radial reflector region, loading 131, the spectrum
has the largest thermal neutron tail of all positions which impacts the reaction rate contribution
as seen in Figure 23. While it is possible that these results can be improved if better B-10 detector
cross sections can be created, it seems rather unrealisitic that this can be done using the existing
version of MC2.

 Figure 24. Normalized Spectrum Results at Each Measurement Position

 Table 41 shows the comparison of the measured values with the DIF3D-VARIANT
calculations where all three described cross section options for drawer type 105 are displayed.
Only location 158-27 in Table 41 has a low enough error to consider DIF3D to be reliably
accurate while the remaining points show a consistent increasing negative bias as the
measurement device was moved outward into the radial reflector, the B 4C/Na shield, and the
simulated pool region. Inspection of both the MCNP and DIF3D results tends to indicate that
both are rather poor in the outer regions. It is important to note that the statistical error in the

 51

MCNP calculation does increase significantly in the ex-core regions making the value calculated
in the sodium pool unreliable. Given the B-10 cross sections are not substantially perturbed when
placed in each region, it is likely that the flux solution produced by DIF3D is causing the
underlying error which means the other isotopic cross sections, or even the deterministic
modeling approach itself, are to blame for the underlying errors. As detailed earlier, the axial
position of the evaluation in DIF3D made little difference in these results and given the MCNP
results are not a perfect match to the experiment, the reason for the errors is likely a mixture of
both identified issues.

9. Analysis of Foil Measurements
 Several types of foils were irradiated in loadings 123 and 134 of ZPPR-15B and loading
203 of ZPPR-15D to measure spatial reaction rate distributions.

9.1 Foil Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loadings 123 and 134
 The reaction rate distribution measurements were performed in two shield
configurations. In the SSNA configuration, Foil irradiation measurements were performed in
loading 123 of ZPPR-15B. In the BCNA configuration, foil measurements were performed in
loading 134 of ZPPR-15B. See Section 7) for a summary description of the SSNA and BCNA
configuration. The keff results for each loading with foils was found to be consistent with the
critical reference results reported earlier.
 In both loading 123 and 134, small metal foils were irradiated to measure reaction rate
distributions along the X-axis in the core, blanket, reflector and shield zones. Four types of foils
were used in the ZPPR-15B shield experiments. U-235(n,f) reactions were measured with U-235
foils. The U-235 foils were 12.7 mm in diameter and 0.112 mm thick. U-238(n,f) and U-238(n,γ)

 52

reactions were measured with U-238 foils. The diameter and thickness of the U-238 foils were
12.7 mm and 0.140 mm, respectively. U-235 and U-238 foils were coated with Kel-F and were
covered on the top and bottom with 0.13 mm aluminum disks. Pu-239 foils were used to measure
Pu-239(n,f) reactions (no Pu-239 foil measurements were performed in L134). The diameter and
thickness of the Pu-239 foils were 10.8 mm and 0.127 mm, respectively, and the nominal
plutonium mass was ~150 mg. The Pu-239 foils were clad in 0.025 mm aluminum. In-115(n,γ)
and (n,n’) reactions were measured with indium foils. The diameter and thickness of the indium
foils were 12.7 mm and 0.25 mm, respectively.
 In Figure 25 and Figure 26, the dashed line indicates the line along which the foils were
placed in half 1 of ZPPR-15B loading 123, the SSNA configuration, and in half 1 of ZPPR-15B
loading 134, the BCNA configuration, respectively.

 Figure 25. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 123 of ZPPR-15B

 53

 Figure 26. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
 The measured foil data was reported in two forms in the ZPPR reports: mapping foil and
cell averaged results [25]. In the present analysis, only the mapping foil data is computed as the
cell averaged data is considered less precise. Details on the location of the mapping foils in the
ZPPR-15 experiments and in the DIF3D geometric model are given in Appendix D. Appendix
D1 provides the measured reaction rates and the positions of the mapping foils in loading 123 of
ZPPR-15B while Appendix D2 provides the measured reaction rates and positions of the
mapping foils in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B.
 The measurements were performed by placing 1 – 3 foils into holes in thin stainless steel
or aluminum holders which were inserted between plates in drawers at selected locations in half
1 of ZPPR-15B. The foils were then irradiated, removed from the reactor and counted to
determine reaction rates. The foils in the holders were centered at the mid-height of the drawer.

 54

The axial positions of the foils from the interface between half 1 and half 2 were 63.1 mm for
the U-235 foils, 77.0 mm for the U-238 foils and 90.8 mm for the Pu-239 foils. When indium
foils were irradiated, they replaced Pu-239 foils at the 90.8 mm position.
 Foil locations were specified for foils placed in the core, blanket, reflector and shield
drawers. For the DIF3D calculation of reaction rates, the MC 2 slab lattice geometries generated
by BuildZPRmodel for core drawers were modified to include narrow foil regions at the specific
locations of the measurement in each drawer type. For the blanket, reflector, and shield drawers,
the foil material was added at infinite dilution to the existing 0D MC 2 input to generate cross
section data. The leakage spectrum methodology was used to connect the domain noting that an
attempt to use a 1D radial model in MC2 failed. In the leakage spectrum methodology, a 0D MC2
model for an active core drawer (type 201) was solved in the conventional k-infinity mode and
a 2082 group leakage spectrum was exported. That leakage spectrum is used as a fixed source
for the blanket region where k-infinity is set to 1.0 and cross sections are obtained. A new leakage
spectrum is exported from the blanket region and passed to the adjacent reflector (SSNA, BCNA,
etc…). In the blanket region, the U-238 present in the drawer is indistinguishable from the foil
and thus the foil cross sections effectively become the drawer U-238 cross section. To get the
calculated reaction rates, the DIF3D flux solution is evaluated at the centroid of the exact foil
location and then combined with the foil cross sections.
 Three normalizations were adopted for the calculated and measured results. In
normalization A, the calculated reaction rate data is normalized by dividing by the sum of the
calculated reaction rate results in the active core and multiplying by the sum of the measured
reaction rate results in the active core. This results in a different normalization for each reaction
type. The sum over the active core was chosen because the foil measurements all have roughly
the same reported measurement error which is typically very low. In normalization B, the sum
of the reaction rate data is taken over all measured and calculated points rather than just the
active core region. In normalization C, the normalization factor calculated for the U-235 fission
reaction rate in normalization A is used to scale all of the other calculated reaction rate results
(i.e. U-238, In-115, etc…).
 With normalization A, the different reaction rates (U-235(n,f), U-238(n,f), U-238 (n,γ),
Pu-239(n,f), In-115(n,γ), and In-115(n,n’)) are not referenced to a single flux level and thus are
relative reaction rate comparisons for a given reaction rate type. Normalization B is just a slightly
different interpretation as the regions with lower flux levels and lower reaction rates are included
in the normalization. For normalization C, all of the reaction rates are all consistently normalized
with the way the measurement was intended and it should expose more problems with the cross
section data and foil cross section generation methodology.
 Table 42 through Table 46 show the calculated results for the reaction rate distributions
in loading 123 of ZPPR-15B. Results for all three normalization schemes are provided along
with the measurement errors. Figure 27 through Figure 31 graph the experimental reaction rate
distribution along with the DIF3D errors for each normalization. In each figure, the reaction rate
values are indexed to the left axis while the DIF3D errors are indexed to the right axis. Each
figure also indicates the regions of the domain where the foils were placed: inner core, outer
core, blanket, reflector, simulated pool (SP), and the SSNA shield region. Starting with the U-
235 fission rate distribution in Table 42 and Figure 27, one can see that the foil distribution has
a very low error in the active core and blanket regions.

However, in the radial reflector and shielding regions, the error rapidly grows to greater than

with the U-235 foil. In both Table 43 and Table 44, the normalization C results are displayed
and one can easily identify an increase in the U-238 fission reaction rate and relatively unchanged

 Figure 31. In-115(n,γ) Reaction Rate Distribution in Loading 123 of ZPPR-15B

 The last set of foils irradiated in this loading are In-115 the results of which are given in
Table 45, Table 46, Figure 30, and Figure 31. In Table 45, the In-115 foils in the reflector and

 60

shield region have a considerably large measurement uncertainty and it is therefore no surprise
to see that the predictions from DIF3D have enormous errors. In the active core, using
normalization scheme A, the errors in both tables are reasonable because of the normalization
scheme itself, but clearly the error rapidly increases in the blanket and shield. Conversely, for
normalization scheme B, the error is generally high everywhere and given the large uncertainties
in the measurement, the normalization scheme is unwise for Table 45. The normalization C
results clearly shows that there is a consistent and large error in the foil cross section compared
that observed with the U-235. It should be noted that the peak of the In-155 capture reaction rate
occurs in the reflector region in DIF3D rather than in the experimentally measured active core
region which clearly highlights a problem with the foil cross sections for DIF3D. This error is
so large that it effectively eliminates the In-115 reaction rate measurements from being usable.
 In summary, the In-115 reaction rate measurements are effectively unusable for
validation work given the inaccuracy in the measurement itself and the inaccuracy of the DIF3D
results. The preceding U-235 and U-238 foils are sufficient to show that the DIF3D reaction rate
predictions are rather accurate in the core and blanket regions with notable poor performance in
the outer reflector and shielding regions. For all three normalizations, the U-235 and U-238
calculated reaction rates had only minor changes in the calculated errors to the experimentally
measured results.
 To better understand the observed discrepancies between the calculated and experimental
U-235(n,f) reaction rates in the reflector, the neutron spectrum and contribution by energy group
was investigated. Figure 32 shows that in the SSNA zone at the interface with the outer core, a
peak in the neutron spectrum is observed at low energies (<1 keV) as expected. This peak
vanishes gradually with respect to outward movement in the SSNA zone. Figure 33 shows the
U-235 foil cross section increases below 1 keV and that there are significant differences at the
two spatial points. Finally, Figure 34 shows that the largest contributions to the U-235(n,f) rate
in the SSNA shield region come from the 10eV – 1000eV energy range. This tends to indicate
that the errors in the shielding region are due to base errors in the U-235 cross section data or
mistakes in the multi-group cross section processing methodology used for this work. No results
using MCNP can be obtained in these regions and thus further analysis is left for future work.

 61

  Figure 33. U-235(n,f) Foil Cross Sections in SSNA and Blanket in Loading 123

 Figure 34. Energy Group Breakdown of the U-235(n,f) Reaction Rate in Loading 123

 Table 47 through Table 52 tabulate the reaction rate results for loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
for all three normalization schemes. Figure 35 through Figure 40 graph the reaction rate
measurement and DIF3D calculated results for loading 134 of ZPPR-15B. As was done for
loading 123, each figure indicates where each measurement lies with respect to the inner core,
outer core, blanket, reflector, simulated pool (SP), SSNA shield, and BCNA shield. The location
of foil measurements was very similar to that used in loading 123 but the changes in shielding
materials (from SSNA to BCNA) makes the measurements quite different.

 Starting with the U-235 fission reaction rate distribution, a quick comparison of Figure
35 with Figure 27 indicates that the errors in the shielding regions are again large. In the core
and blanket regions the DIF3D results are still accurate and additional follow-on work using
different space-angle approximations did not appreciably change the calculated foil
measurement results.

 Continuing with the U-238 foil results in Table 48, Table 49, Figure 36, and Figure 37,
one can see that the fission reaction rate measurements are accurate in the inner core, outer core,
and blanket regions but considerable errors are found in the shield and reflector regions. The
capture reaction rate results are also rather good with less than 7% errors for all three
normalization schemes but the foils were only placed in the core and blanket regions.

 No Pu-239 foil results were made in loading 123 but they are present for loading 134. As
was the case with U-238 capture foils, the Pu-239 fission foils were only placed in the core
region. From Figure 38 one can see that the Pu-239 reaction rate prediction is quite accurate (less
than 1% of error). The normalization C result indicates a slight consistent bias in the Pu-239
versus U-235 fission rates but overall this is a minor concern.

 Figure 37. U-238(n,γ) Reaction Rate Distribution in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
 The In-115 reaction rate distributions were also included in loading 134. In both loading
123 and 134, very few foils were placed in the inner core and outer core regions and it would
have been much more valuable to place those foils in the core to get a better handle on the base
level of accuracy of the In-115 reaction rates. As detailed in Figure 39 and Figure 40, the
normalization C option indicates a very strong bias in the In-115 cross sections compared with
the U-235 fission one which makes the DIF3D calculated results inherently untrustable. While

 66

the normalization A option does indicate some consistency for the In-115(n,γ) reaction in the
active core, the (n,n’) reaction has very large errors compared with all of the other foil results in
the active core regions. As was the case with loading 123, the loading 134 In-115 foils are rather
unusable because of the inaccuracy of the DIF3D calculated result and the poorer quality of the
measurement.

 Overall, the calculated reaction rate distributions in loading 134 are good for the core and
blanket regions and rather poor for the remaining regions. Combined with 123 and the neutron
spectrum measurements from earlier in this manuscript, these results indicate that DIF3D is
doing well at predicting the flux distribution within the core and blanket regions. The reflector
and shield regions are not as good noting that propagation of the errors in the reflector into outer
regions could be partly to blame for the errors in the outer regions.

 Figure 40. In-115(n,γ) Reaction Rate Distribution in Loading 134 of ZPPR-15B
 As was done for loading 123, the neutron spectrum and group wise contribution for the
U-235(n,f) reaction rate was studied. Figure 41 displays the neutron spectra in several parts of
the geometry. As can be seen, the SP, SSNA, and BCNA regions have a peak in the neutron
spectrum at low energies (~1 keV) as expected. The tail on that peak vanishes quickly in the
BCNA zone as the B-10 absorbs the neutrons but reappears in the sodium pool outside of that.
Figure 42 shows that the U-235 fission cross section increases below 1 keV. Figure 43 shows
the group-wise breakdown of the U-235 fission rate and one can see that the SP and SSNA region

 69

closest to the reactor has a substantial contribution below 1 keV while the remaining points have
more balanced contributions over the 1 eV to 1 MeV range. As was the case for loading 123, this
indicates that the errors in the shielding region are due to base errors in the U-235 cross section
data or mistakes in the multi-group cross section processing methodology used for this work. No
results using MCNP can be obtained in these regions and thus further analysis is left for future
work.

 Figure 42. U-235 Foil Cross Section at Several Regions of Loading 134

 Figure 43. Energy Group Contribution at Several Locations in Loading 134

9.2 Foil Measurements in ZPPR-15D Loadings 203
 Foil measurements were also done for loading 203 of ZPPR-15D. Unlike loading 123
and 134, loading 203 included reaction rate distributions along the X, Y, and Z axes in the core,
radial blanket and radial reflector. The axial (Z) reaction rate distributions were measured at
three locations labeled Z1, Z2, and Z3. The same U-235(n,f), U-238(n,f) and U-238(n,γ), and
Pu-239 (n,f) foils were used in these measurements using the same loading methodology. Figure
44 shows the general placement of foils in the X and Y directions and the three drawers chosen
for the Z direction measurements. All foils were placed in half 1.

 71

 Figure 44. Reaction Rate Traverses in Loading 203 of ZPPR-15D

 Similar to loading 123 and 134, the measured foil data were reported in basic and cell
average values. In the present analysis, only the basic data are analyzed for which the measured
reaction rates and positions of the foils are provided in Appendix D3. The same three
normalization strategies used for loading 123 and 134 were used here. Table 53 through Table
72 tabulate the reaction rate distribution results for all foils used in loading 203 where the error
from using the three normalizations schemes is displayed. Because Pu-239 foils were irradiated,
the common spectral indices can be computed at some of the foil positions which are tabulated
in Table 73 through Table 87. The spectral indices included are the U-238(n,f)/Pu-239(n,f), U-
238(n,γ)/ Pu-239(n,f) and U-235(n,f)/Pu-239(n,f) reaction rate ratios where it is assumed that the
foils underwent the same irradiation conditions and normalization C is used.
 Figure 45 through Figure 64 plot the reaction rate distribution results in Table 53 through
Table 72 for the three normalization schemes applied to the DIF3D results and are presented

 72

next to the corresponding table of output they are derived from. In each figure, the experiment
reaction rate measurements are indexed to the left axis while the error in the DIF3D results are
indexed to the right axis. Each figure for the X axis traverse indicates the reactor region where
the foil measurement was located. The Y traverse does not have a region detail as it is rather
obvious that the error distribution in the X traverse will match the Y traverse with respect to the
where the error peaks. No such detail is given in the Z traverse results for the same basic
reasoning.

 From Table 53 through Table 72, the DIF3D results for the Pu-239(n,f) foils are almost
all within the 3-sigma measurement error with just the last few points in the Z traverses just
beyond it. It is worth noting that the normalization C methodology (normalize with respect to
the U-235 fission measurement), has a consistently worse error and that normalization A and B
(normalization with respect to all or part of the Pu-239 fission measurement itself) is
systematically better. In past studies [28] the uncertainty on the calculated value of a normalized
reaction rate in the core of a typical sodium fast reactor was estimated to be on the order of 1%,

 73

without accounting for the uncertainty from the foil cross sections. For the same reason, the Pu-
239 reaction rate distribution along the Z direction at Z3 (outer core boundary) show slightly
larger discrepancies compared with the reaction rate distribution at Z1 (inner core). Finally, the
measured Pu-239 reaction rate distribution along the X axis in Figure 45, has a small drop in the
measured result at drawer 903 which is not reproduced by DIF3D (error peaks). Given no
meaningful investigation can be done to understand why this occurred, the calculated result
would seem more believable given the drawer contents of 902 and 903 are effectively identical.
Most likely what has occurred is that the measured values for the two 916 drawers after the 903
drawer were misordered by the experimenters.

 Figure 49. Pu-239(n,f) Reaction Rates Along the Z-Axis at Z3 for Loading 203
 Next are the U-238(n,f) reaction rate distributions in Table 58 through Table 62 that are
plotted in Figure 50 through Figure 54. As was the case with the U-238(n,f) reaction rate
distributions in loading 123 and 134, the DIF3D results agree well with the measured values in
the core with a maximum error less than 10% for the A and B normalizations. Normalization C
consistently has more error than A or B. It is worth noting that a majority of the actual errors are
less than 5% for normalization A and B. The error in the DIF3D results increases significantly
in the blanket region having a maximum of ~70% at the outermost measurement in Z3 and
generally greater than 5% in all of the measurements. The Z axis traverses in Figure 52 through
Figure 54 show larger errors than the X and Y axis measurements, but the measurement error
also increases significantly reducing the confidence in the blanket measurements.
 Similar to the Pu-239 fission measurements, there are abberattions in the X and Y axis
measurements that are traced to drawers 916, 923, 925, 926 and 928. The measurement error is
noted to increase in these drawers although the overall impact is minor. In these measurements,
the DIF3D results mimic the measurement peaks and close inspection shows that these drawers
have two fuel columns instead of a single fuel column in the adjacent 901, 902, and 903 drawers.
As a consequence, the measurement peaks correspond to the locations where the fuel content
increases and thus it makes sense which was not the case for the Pu-239f foils where a dip
occurred in between a 902 and 916 drawer for no apparent reason.

The C normalization results are notably similar to the A and B normalization results for these

furthest extent of Z3. The trends here are considerably different from the U-238(n,f) results.
While the DIF3D results still have significant errors in the blanket region, overall, they are rather
acceptable.

 Figure 60 and Figure 61 display the X and Y axis U-235(n,f) reaction rate distribution
results. As was the case for the U-235(n,f) in loading 123 and 134 of ZPPR-15B, the U-235(n,f)
in ZPPR-15D results are very accurate in the core and blanket regions and the errors increase
considerably in the radial reflector region. As was concluded for the previous loadings, the U-
235(n,f) foil cross sections are expected to be reasonably accurate and the flux magnitude and
spectrum are believed to be incorrect because of problems with the stainless steel cross sections.
It is worth noting that an abberation occurs with the foil measurement in Figure 61 at drawer 901
which is next to similarly loaded drawers 902. There is again no explanation for this difference
and the DIF3D predictions do not replicate this dip and thus experimental measurement error is
likely higher than that specified in Table 69 for this measurement.

 Figure 62 through Figure 64 display the Z axis traverse results at the three locations in
Figure 44. As can be seen, the results are consistent with the X and Y axis results with an
excellent match in the core region and slightly worse results in the blanket and reflector regions.
Overall, these results are acceptable for DIF3D.

 The spectral indices results displayed in Table 73 through Table 87 are part of the
reported measurement data. These results are based upon normalization C and the errors are
notably similar to those of the individual reaction rate data. Because most of the available data
is in the core region, the errors are less than 10% in the core regions and less than 25% in the
blanket regions.

 As was done in loading 123 and 134, analysis of the neutron spectrum and the energy
group contributions to the U-235(n,f) reaction rate were carried out at a few measurement

 97

locations. Figure 65 plots the spectrum results at each measurement point where BK is blanket
and RF is the radial reflector zone at the interface with the blanket, a peak in the neutron spectrum
is observed at low energies (<1 keV) consistent with the previous results. This peak reduces
gradually with outward movement towards the reflector zone. Figure 66 shows that the U-235
foil fission cross section increases below 1 keV and is relatively consistent between the inner
and outer core. Finally, Figure 67 shows that the largest contributions to the U235(n,f) reaction
rate in the radial reflector are located in the 10eV – 1 keV energy range. This result is also
consistent with the previous results and indicates that the most likely cause for the errors is the
flux distribution more than the multi-group U-235 cross section. No effort was made to
reproduce any of these results using MCNP given the computational effort required to complete
the task.

 Except for the In-115 foils from ZPPR-15B, all of the preceding foil measurement
comparisons indicate that the DIF3D calculations are accurate within the core region for cross
sections that are well known. Combined with the spectrum measurements, this work can be used
to validate the accuracy of the flux spectrum and neutron power distribution obtained with
DIF3D.
10.Analysis of Pu-242 and Np-237 Fission Rate Measurements
 Back-to-back flowing-gas ionization chambers were used to measure the threshold
fission rates of Np-237 and Pu-242 in loading 141 of ZPPR-15B. The chambers were those
described elsewhere [25]. Two chambers, each with one Pu-242 deposit and one Np-237 deposit,
were irradiated simultaneously, one in the sodium pool outside the shield and one in the radial
blanket in the side of the assembly away from the shield. The chambers were accommodated in
drawers by removing most of the materials from the first 152 mm of the drawers. A 12.7 mm
wide and a 12.7 mm high channel was also provided for cables and gas tubes. Fission rates in
the Pu-242 and Np-237 were determined by correcting the total chamber counts for counts
contributed by small amounts of fissionable impurities in the deposits. The deposit compositions
were determined in mass spectrometric and alpha counting measurements. Fission rates for the
impurity isotopes were provided by calculations. The deposits are sufficiently thin that infinitely
dilute cross sections are appropriate for calculations comparisons with measurements.

 For the Np-237 and Pu-242 rates, uncertainties were estimated based upon counting
statistics and the ability to separate counts due to fission events from counts due to alpha
emission. Uncertainties in the impurity corrections to the Pu-242 rates were assumed to be 10%
of the correction, which was small compared to other uncertainty sources. The estimated
uncertainties for neptunium were 2% in the blanket and 3.6% outside the shield. For plutonium,
they were 1.5% in the blanket and 5% outside the shield. The Np-237 and Pu-242 threshold

 99

fission reaction rates were measured in the sodium pool (SP) outside of the shield (location 158-
19) and in the radial blanket (RB) on the side of the assembly away from the shield (location
158-67), as shown in Figure 139 of Appendix A. The k eff result for this loading is consistent with
the critical reference loading for ZPPR-15B. The measured rates and calculated values with
DIF3D are given in Table 88 along with the location of the chambers in the ZPPR-15B loading
and deterministic model (see Appendix D for details).

 The measured Pu-242 values in Table 88 have been corrected for contributions to the
count rate from Pu-239, Pu-240, and Pu-241 which were present in the deposit in small
concentrations. The largest correction, about 4%, was from Pu-241 in the simulated sodium pool
location all of which were based upon calculated reaction rates. The total corrections were ~5%
in the sodium pool and ~2% in the blanket. No corrections were needed for the Np-237
measurements. Because there is insufficient information to compute an absolute reaction rate,
the ratio of the two measurements is used to compare the results and is included in Table 88. As
can be seen, the measurement indicates a similar ratio between the Pu-242 and Np-237 results.
The DIF3D result also produces a similar ratio for the two isotopes, although clearly not as
consistent as the measured one, but the magnitude of that ratio is ~60% below the ratio of the
measured values. This outcome and actual type of measurement (shielding) tends to make this
measurement not useful for validation purposes noting that the effort required to complete this
task is trivial compared with the preceding foil reaction rates.
 To better understand the observed discrepancies the neutron spectrum and energy group
contribution to the Pu-242(n,f) and Np-237(n,f) reaction rates was analyzed. Figure 68 displays

 100

the DIF3D neutron spectrum at each measurement location and indicates a significant
thermalization of the neutron spectrum in the simulated pool.

 Figure 68. DIF3D Flux Spectrum at the Measurement Locations in Loading 141
Figure 69 displays the foil cross sections for the two isotopes which have minor differences
around 50 eV. Finally, Figure 70 displays the energy group contribution to the total reaction rate
in the blanket which demonstrates that the largest contributions to the isotopic reaction rate
comes from high neutron energies >20keV. This can be contrasted with the energy group
breakdown in the sodium pool shown in Figure 71 where there is a non-negligible contribution
at ~1 keV. Given the preceding foil analysis similar to this indicated problems with the flux
magnitude in the sodium pool, it is likely that this aspect also dominates the error observed in
this calculation.

11. Analysis of Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth Measurements
 Axial expansion experiments were performed in ZPPR-15B and ZPPR-15D. In each
case a segmented drawer was constructed. The back of the segmented drawer was connected to
a spring-loaded cable that could be pulled away from the axial core centerline to introduce gaps
between drawer segments. Releasing the cable eliminated the gaps between drawer segments,
so the segmented drawer was essentially identical to any other core drawer when the cable was
released. The small gaps introduced between drawer segments simulated core axial expansion
as closely as possible given the constraint that the core materials consisted of rigid plates of fixed
sizes. The segmented drawer design allowed experimenters to measure the reactivity worths of
the reference and expanded configurations in one reactor run.

 102

 The ZPPR-15B and ZPPR-15D experiments both had a base configuration with no gaps
and one or multiple perturbed configurations where gaps between sections of each drawer were
opened. Figure 72 shows a pictorial representation of a standard core drawer (upper picture), the
base case of the axial expansion drawer (center), and the perturbed or expanded case of the
expansion drawer (lower). As can be seen, with every drawer there is a core, blanket, and
reflector region, the dimensions of which can be found in the ZPPR-15 experiment details [21]
to [26].

 Figure 72. Visualization of the Axial Expansion Drawer in Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

An existing core drawer was cut into several pieces, small stainless steel plates (0.0762 cm
thickness for ZPPR-15B, 0.0508 cm thickness for ZPPR-15D) were attached to the open ends of
each segment to prevent axial shifting of the plates during the experiment. This causes the axial
expansion drawer to have a slight misalignment at each major region boundary compared with
the standard core drawer geometry.

11.1 Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth of Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

 The segmented fuel drawer for loading 117 of ZPPR-15B is divided into three parts, as
shown in Figure 72. The first segment is the first 6 inches of the core while the second segment
is the next 6 inches of the core (6 – 12 in). The third segment is the remainder of the core region
(12 – 18 in) plus the axial blanket (18 – 31 in) and axial reflector (31 – 36 in). All of the fuel
plates used for the core in the segmented drawer were ~6 inches long. The plates used for the
axial blanket and axial reflector in the segmented drawer were consistent with respect to the
materials loaded in adjacent core drawers.

 For the expansion experiment in loading 117, the size of gap between each segment was
0.25 inches. Modeling the gap explicitly is difficult for DIF3D as it cannot handle “void” regions.
In order to use the transport options of DIF3D, one easy solution is to introduce a small smearing
zone at each axial boundary, as illustrated in Figure 73. In this scheme, four axial regions are
added to the typical three region homogenization approach. The axial size of the four
homogenization zones (A2, A4, A6 and A8) are user defined and the values in Table 89 were
used for this work. As seen, five models were constructed and analyzed. The segment boundary
of the expanded drawer is shifted ~0.5 inches from the original core boundary, and because it is
desirable to keep the boundary consistent with the current homogenization zone the minimum
homogenization zone should be no less than 0.5 inch.

 103

  Figure 73. Auxiliary Homogenization Zones Introduced for Modeling the Axial Expansion

 Table 89. Auxiliary Homogenization Zone Sizing for the Axial Expansion Experiment in
 Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B.
 Axial Length (inches)
 Model 1 Model 2 Model 3 Model 4 Model 5
                  A2     2.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A4     2.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A6     1.0         1.0           0.75           0.6         0.52
                  A8       1.0        1.0           0.75            0.6        0.52

 The default input to BuildZPRmodel is the un-expanded configuration of this drawer.
Thus, with proper input, BuildZPRmodel can calculate the atom densities of all of the axial
regions of this drawer (i.e. A1 to A10) directly for use in the DIF3D model of the full core case.
For the expanded configuration, the introduction of a 0.25 inch gap at boundary 1 and 2 and 0.5
inch gap at boundary 3 and 4 can again be applied to the unexpanded configuration setup. Region
specific cross section data was not generated for the expanded model, but the number densities
were adjusted and the isotopic cross section data from the unexpanded model were used.

 To facilitate the calculation of the expanded drawer configuration, dummy ZPPR
problems were created and used in BuildZPRmodel. These dummy problems were only used to
produce the atom density results for each homogenized zone and do not make any physical sense
with regard to a ZPPR assembly. With respect to Figure 73, at boundaries 1 and 2, the dummy
problem introduces a homogenization zone that is 0.25 inch smaller such that the total mass of
the expanded drawer can be preserved properly. At boundaries 3 and 4, the dummy model uses
a homogenization zone that is 0.5 inch smaller. For clarity, the unexpanded and expanded drawer
model both needs to merge some of the material from zone A5 (core) with zone A6 (blanket). In
the dummy model, the boundary is moved to properly obtain the homogenized composition for
the expanded configuration.

 Using the five models described above, Table 90 provides the calculated axial expansion
reactivity worth for loading 117 of ZPPR-15B where the measured result is -0.641 pcm ± 5.87%.
As can be seen, as the size of the homogenization region is reduced, the accuracy of the predicted
worth improves. At the very finest two models (0.6 and 0.52 inches), the error is observed to
increase beyond the experimental uncertainty indicating that one can potentially get a much

 104

worse answer if a fully heterogeneous calculation was attempted. The impact of using different
energy group structures and angular approximations had little impact on the DIF3D results and
other calculations using different spatial approximations (not shown for brevity) yielded a similar
conclusion.

 Based upon the preceding results, model 4 is closest to the measurement even though
model 5 should be more accurate as it is more rigorous with regard to the material movement.
This indicates that there is a limit to the homogenization methodology and there likely is some
cancellation of error occurring with the homogenization itself. After some study, the movement
of fuel materials into the void region was determined to be the primary problem and this is not
accurate with regard to the underlying expansion experiment.

 To remedy the situation, two smaller homogenization zones are introduced at boundaries
1 and 2, termed Model 6 in this report. Given well selected homogenization zone sizing, the
artificial movement of fuel (the high worth part of the structure being moved) material into the
gapped region can be minimized for both the un-expanded and expanded configurations. This of
course introduces smaller mesh sizes and constructs a model which is closer to the real
heterogeneous problem (the components of the steel drawer end plates are still smeared into the
gapped regions). Figure 74 illustrates the final homogenization methodology utilized for the
axial expansion experiment in loading 117 of ZPPR-15B.

 105

 Figure 74. Model 6 Homogenization Scheme for Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

 The axial boundaries used in the DIF3D model are provided in Table 91 where the
smallest mesh size is 0.250 inches. For this model, the transport options of DIF3D converge
when using a 33 group cross section library but do not when using a 116 group cross section
library thus diffusion theory results are provided. The DIF3D axial expansion reactivity worth
results are tabulated in Table 92. As can be seen, the results for all of the calculations are
consistent and ~15% above the measured results. It is not clear if this error is due to the
homogenization or other modeling issues (i.e. cross section data).

 As stated, even when using Model 6, the existing model of the gap still involves smearing
of the structural materials (parts of drawer and some plate cladding) into the gap regions. Thus,
two heterogeneous models were attempted: Model 7 and 8. Model 7 focuses on strict
preservation of the material boundaries but assumes the fuel material is basically the same over
the active core while model 8 exactly preserves the intended compositions in their correct
locations for both expanded and non-expanded configurations. For these explicit models, each
segment of the drawer is homogenized to form a single composition and thus have identical
compositions in the expanded and unexpanded case. In the expanded case, the matrix tube

 106

composition is simply inserted using the region specific cross sections needed to define the
content. For model 1 through 7, the results were obtained using the inserted aluminum plate
methodology while model 8 exclusively uses the FAKEPA isotope inclusion as detailed earlier.
 Table 92. Model 6 Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth for Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B
 Angular Angular Reactivity

 Flux Scattering Worth Error

                                      3              1          -0.74      15.4%
                          33          3              3          -0.75      17.0%
                                     Diffusion Theory           -0.75      17.0%
                         116         Diffusion Theory           -0.76      18.6%

 Adding new spatial boundaries between the expanded and unexpanded models is
equivalent to spatial mesh refinement. In general, this would be a minor impact on the overall
solution and would not be a concern for problems such as a control rod insertion. In this particular
case, the worth of the expansion is so small that spatial mesh refinement was observed to
introduce substantial errors into the solution. Thus, both expanded and unexpanded models must
utilize the identical axial meshing even though the unexpanded case does not require those
additional mesh boundaries. Table 93 provides the axial boundaries used for the two
configurations of the expandable drawer for better clarity on the explicit modeling. In the original
work (using an aluminum plate based modification), DIF3D was only able to solve this problem
using transport with a 33 group cross section library where all higher energy group libraries
diverged and only diffusion theory solutions were possible. In the updated approach with
FAKEPA, both diffusion and transport work and only the 230 group transport result is displayed,
consistent with the rest of the models in this manuscript.
 The calculated reactivity worth and error with respect to the measurement is shown in
Table 94. Comparing of the results for Model 7 with Model 6 (the homogenized boundary case)
results in Table 92, one can see that they are very consistent when using transport but quite
inconsistent when using diffusion theory. Thus Model 6 is likely more reliable for diffusion
theory which is known to have difficulties with large material changes. Model 8 is particularly
accurate with respect to the measurement and is the most rigorous geometric approach that can
be taken in DIF3D and thus some additional work was done to assess the extrapolation value
with respect to axial expansion. Figure 75 shows the reactivity worth results for loading 117
using 230 group transport with gap sizes ranging from the experiment value of 0.25 inches to 0.5
inches. The red line shows the linear extrapolation result which, when using all four values
produces a slightly higher reactivity worth than the calculated result at 0.25. If the last three
results are exclusively used to define the extrapolation, they would also produce a slightly more
negative result than the calculated one. In both cases, the extrapolated value is consistent with
the calculated one and we can consider this not to be an abberation, but an exceptionally accurate
calculated result given the one-sigma uncertainty on the measurement is at least 5%.

 Beyond the demonstrated extrapolated results from model 8, the error in the DIF3D
results for the other models are difficult to confirm and past experience with DIF3D would
suggest that it should produce an excellent result as the expansion itself constitutes a minor
change in the flux distribution. In this regard, the actual worth is caused by the movement of the
material rather than a 1st or 2nd order change in the flux shape. Inspection of the differences
between model 7 and 8 indicate that the model 7 assumption that the fuel composition in the first
two drawer segments is identical to the last one is the source of the difference. Because of the
difference in material content, one must apply one of the homogenization models (1-6) or
utilitize model 8 where clearly model 8 is the more reliable approach as it introduces minimal
additional approximations. In addition, in the older models where aluminum was added to the
empty matrix tube portion of the drawers, this constitutes the inclusion of material into the gaps
which makes the response more negative. The same thing happens with the FAKEPA, although
to a lesser degree, and to eliminate this aspect, the FAKEPA was added to all compositions of
this drawer in both expanded and un-expanded configurations, thereby yielding the good result
shown above.

 108

 Figure 75. Extrapolation Behavior of Model 8 for Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B

11.2 Axial Expansion Reactivity Worth for Loading 206 of ZPPR-15D

 In ZPPR-15D, the expandable drawer was segmented into nine pieces and three different
experiments were done with it, as illustrated in Figure 76. The nominal segment length was ~2
inches for each of the first eight core segments. The remaining ~2 inches of the core, ~13 inches
of axial blanket and ~5 inches of axial reflector were placed in the ninth segment which had a
total length of ~20 inches. There was a 0.020 in (0.0508 cm) steel plate fixed to the open end of
each segment to prevent axial motion of the plates in each segment. Based upon the results for
Loading 117 of ZPPR-15B, only Model 7 and 8 are used with DIF3D when computing the axial
expansion worth of Loading 206 of ZPPR-15D.
 As stated, three axial expansion experiments were performed with two of them
introducing 2 gaps and one introducing 8 gaps. In the 2 gap cases, the width of the gaps is
different thereby resulting in a different reactivity worth for the two experiments. With the
explicit modeling of the boundaries, the first experiment is the hardest to deal with as it
introduces the most boundaries. Table 95 tabulates the axial boundaries used to construct Model
7 and 8 for Loading 206. As can be seen, both the expanded and un-expanded configurations use
the same axial meshing to avoid spatial mesh refinement contamination in the computed results.
Similarly, Table 96 and Table 97 provide the axial meshing used to construct Model 7 and 8 for
the two experiments that introduce two gaps.

 109

 Figure 76. Three Axial Expansion Experiments for Loading 206 of ZPPR-15D

 The three measured worths for experiment 1, 2, and 3 are -0.689 pcm ± 5.83%, -0.707
pcm ± 5.96%, and -0.463 pcm ± 5.90%, respectively. As was the case with Loading 117, it is
not possible to compute these expansion measurements with MCNP. DIF3D was not able to
produce a transport result with the aluminum insertion methodology using an energy group
structure greater than 33 groups for two of the experiments in Loading 206. For the 8 gap
problem, DIF3D was unable to produce a transport result for Model 7 for any of the preferred
energy group structures and thus only diffusion results are possible. For Model 8, the FAKEPA
methodology was used and thus both diffusion theory and transport theory results were
obtainable in 230 groups.

 Table 98 provides the results computed using DIF3D for all three experiments using
Model 7 and 8. As can be seen, for experiment 2, a transport solution was possible with DIF3D
using 116 groups with Model 7. Because the diffusion theory results are known to be unreliable,
the larger errors for all three experiments are of no surprise. Looking at the expansion results in
loadings 117 and 206, it is rather clear that using diffusion theory over predicts the reactivity
worth which is indicative of the diffusion theory methodology over predicting leakage on a wide
variety of past reactor analysis cases. Focusing on just the transport results, the Model 7 results
are clearly inferior to the Model 8 results and the latter are nearly within the one-sigma error bars
of the experiment in all of the expansion models.

 In conclusion, the single homogenization methodology (Model 1 to 5) is not wise and
should not be used for these types of expansion calculations. While the two homogenization zone
model (Model 6) can be accurate, it is more difficult to setup and thus simply easier to build
Model 7 or 8 which preserves the material content. Assuming simplifications of the experiment,
as was done in model 7, are clearly unacceptable for reproducing the experimental measurement
and thus model 8 is the best approach. The calculated error with respect to the measurement for
model 8 is within the uncertainty bars on Loading 117 and nearly there with all three
measurements in Loading 206. Further, the Loading 117 results indicate the FAKEPA based
approach yields a consistent extrapolated value from larger gap sizes and thus no further work
is required for confirmation or sources of error on any of these expansion models.

12.Analysis of Doppler Sample Reactivity Worth Measurements
 Whole core Doppler reactivity worth measurements were not feasible in ZPPR because
a) measuring the ZPPR temperature distribution accurately would have required a very large
number of thermocouples, b) it was not possible to perform the calculations required to account
for thermal expansion of the plates, drawers and matrix tubes resulting from heating and c) safety
considerations would have limited the allowed heating. Doppler sample reactivity worth
measurements were performed in ZPPR-15A, ZPPR-15B, and ZPPR-15D [23]. Unlike whole
core Doppler reactivity worth, the Doppler sample allowed for much more controlled
measurements of the Doppler worth as the temperature change of the sample could be accurately
measured. In the experiment, a Doppler sample was loaded into the Doppler mechanism which
consisted of a heating element surrounded by an outer capsule. The temperature of the sample
was measured with a thermocouple inserted into a small cylindrical cavity in the center of the
sample. The Doppler mechanism was placed in a tray that itself was placed in a matrix tube at
the designated core location. The tray was long enough to extend through both halves of the
matrix. The worth of the sample was measured by measuring the reactivity change as the
Doppler mechanism and sample were oscillated into and out of the center of the core. The worth
of the heating element, capsule and other components of the mechanism was measured by
repeating the measurement without the Doppler sample present in the mechanism. The
difference between the measured worth with and without the Doppler sample determines the
worth of just the Doppler sample itself. Measurements were obtained at various temperatures
between 300 K and ~1100 K.

 114

 Figure 77 shows a cross-sectional view of the Doppler sample, equipment and tray.
Figure 78 shows the a cross-sectional view of just the Doppler sample. Four Doppler samples
were measured in ZPPR-15 although the specific set of samples measured in each phase was
different. Doppler sample N3 consisted of natural UO2, sample N11 consisted of depleted
uranium metal, sample N24 consisted of depleted U-10Zr (depleted uranium metal alloyed with
10 wt.% Zr), and sample E33-A consisted of 33% enriched UO2. Each sample had a diameter
of 2.54 cm. Samples N3, N24 and E33-A were 30.5 cm long while sample N11 was 25.4 cm
long. Samples N3 and N11 were measured in loading 43 of ZPPR-15A. Samples N3, N11, and
N24 were measured in loading 139 of ZPPR-15B. All four samples were measured in loading
207 of ZPPR-15D. Further details on the samples can be found in reference [23].

 Figure 77. Cross-Sectional View of Doppler Experimental Equipment

 Figure 78. Cross-Sectional View of a Doppler Sample

 115

 While BuildZPRmodel produces MC2 inputs for all regions in the DIF3D model, the
generation of the Doppler sample cross sections requires additional effort. The input to
BuildZPRmodel included a dummy Doppler sample drawer. Because BuildZPRmodel does not
deal with different temperatures (only 300K), it cannot directly build the MC 2 input for the
Doppler sample drawer (1002). After some testing, the most accurate method to obtain multi-
group cross sections for the Doppler sample involved creating a 1D radial model in MC 2. The
inner cylinder would be the homogenized drawer 1002 mixture while the surrounding annulus
would be a fully homogenized composition from the drawers surrounding the 1002 drawer in
the real loading. The central cylinder has an ~3.12 cm radius and the remainder of the geometry
was arbitrarily set to 50 cm to ensure the 3.12 cm section was of little importance to the total
flux solution. The loading details of each ZPPR-15A, -15B, and -15D were inspected and a single
drawer type was typically present surrounding the Doppler sample location.

 For ZPPR-15A, drawer type 1002 was surrounded by drawer type 102. This drawer input
was taken from the full core BuildZPRmodel input and used to create a 2x2 loading that
BuildZPRmodel could process to construct homogenized MC 2 models (of drawer 102 and 1002).
The BuildZPRmodel inputs for drawer 1002 for the N03, N11, N24, and E33 Doppler samples
were constructed by preserving the cylindrical area with squares as shown in Figure 79 (matrix
tube, Doppler sample drawer, and Doppler sample are displayed. This aspect is immaterial to the
actual MC2 cross section model other than being the means to exactly obtain the fully
homogenized compositions put into the 1D radial MC 2 model.

 Figure 79. Cross-Sectional View of the BuildZPRmodel Drawer Input

 It should be noted that the reported worths by the experimenters were given in cents/kg
where kg refers to the mass of U-238 in the sample. The atom densities of the samples and the
total mass of U-238 in each sample was provided while the total measured mass of the sample
was not. This is a combination of very poor decisions as the measurement itself was done in
cents and the atom densities, combined with the reported dimensions of the samples, yield
inconsistencies with the reported mass of U-238 as detailed in Table 99. It should also be noted
that the mass density of the samples computed with the stated atom densities is not consistent
with the known mass densities of the described compositions. For example, for samples N03 and
E33, the UO2 density using the reported atom densities is 8.25 g/cm 3 and 7.45 g/cm3, respectively
where the correct density should be closer to 10 g/cm 3. The N11 and N24 samples have densities
reasonably similar to those expected, but the N24 sample has a reported U-238 mass which is
well below the mass calculable with the given atom density information. Arbitrarily dividing all
of the measured results by some calculated U-238 mass, or really any computed quantity, directly

 116

causes problems with the modeling effort here. Other issues with the results are that the
experimentalists corrected the temperature results based upon a fit of the measured data to
account for phase changes along with corrections for axial expansion of the sample.

 With all of the preceding problems outlined, for the analysis work here, the mass values
reported by the experimentalists are assumed to be those used to create the cents/kg reported
measured results. The fact that there is a disagreement in mass when using the atom densities
provided by the experimentalists and the sample dimensions is assumed to be a uncorrected
mistake by the experimentalists when reporting their results. In effect we assume the
experimentalists measured the total sample mass and calculated atom densities that were
consistent with that mass and the geometry they assumed for their model which they provided
dimensions on. The disagreement of the provided atom densities with those resulting from
typical densities of those materials is assumed to be a result of voids or other non-reported
aspects of the Doppler samples which decrease the effective material density. In the end, the
error in the mass can be considered translatable to an error in the measured worth. The worst
error is for N24 which is 10% while the rest are less than 2%.

 The total sample worth was measured at 300K and reported in each loading. The DIF3D
calculated worth and the measured worth of all samples in ZPPR-15A (loading 43), ZPPR-15B
(loading 139), and ZPPR-15D (loading 207) are provided in Table 100 for a range of space-
angle-energy approximations in DIF3D. The spatial approximation notation used previously of
F6-Z4-S6-L1 is replaced here with the DIF3D input of 60621. The errors on the measured worth
were modified from the original reported errors to include a 5% error associated with the
uncertainty in β that is used as part of the conversion of measured reactivity versus calculated
reactivity and the error from the sample reported mass detailed above. This latter aspect is not
statistical and thus the modified experimental errors reported in Table 100 should not be viewed
as fully statistical in nature. Because only sample N24 has a large error in the reported mass, the

 117

modified error provided in in Table 100 effectively becomes the 5% error assumed in the β unit
conversion.

 Starting with the N03 sample in Table 100, one finds that the sample worth is rather
consistent for the three loadings but the error profile is not. The DIF3D computed results for
loading 139 are considerably worse than the other two loadings where the measurement error of
5%. What is interesting to note is that the sample has a non-trivial dependence upon the angular
approximation. For the P3P1 to P5P3 transition at 33 groups, the DIF3D calculated result is
observed to increase by 4-5%. The P3P1 results for 33, 116, and 230 groups are all very similar
for all of the samples indicating that the group structure is likely not a driving factor in the worth
calculation. The importance of the angular approximation is likely due to the fact that a bulk of
the Doppler sample drawer position is filled with void and thus there is non-trivial leakage for
the Doppler sample worth calculation.

 139). The N11 sample results are notably better for all loadings with errors mostly near or within
 the 5% error reported for the measurement. The N24 sample has the largest modified
 measurement error due to the error in the reported mass and the computed errors all turn out to

 well within the modified measurement error.

 Looking at the N3 Doppler sample worth, the comparison is rather good with less than
 10% error for the bulk of the DIF3D results. The highest error is 12.4% and using the highest

 119

 energy group structure one gets errors below 9.6%. The 33 group results have errors in the 3-7%
 range. The spatial approximation change at 116 groups has almost no impact on the worth
 calculations. It is unusual to see the anisotropic scattering kernel have an impact on the
 calculated worth but this is clearly occuring for the N3, 116 group structure results. It is noted
 that the 33 group structure shows a much smaller dependence on the anisotropic scattering and
 the N11 result for 116 groups shows less of an impact. The actual errors in the calculated worth
 are on the order of ~0.01 pcm which is very small for a typical eigenvalue problem with DIF3D
 and thus does not warrant attempting higher space-angle-energy approximations for the purposes
 of this report.

 Continuing with the N11 sample results, the errors with respect to the reported results are
 mostly similar to those seen with the N3 sample but still well within 10% on the bulk of the
 results. Combined, the N3 and N11 sample results can be considered accurate enough given the
 very small worth of the actual measurement, the non-trivial measurement and unit conversion
 errors.

 Compared with the sample worth results from loading 43, the DIF3D results are rather
 similar, but the errors are consistently smaller for all of the higher temperture measurements.
 The largest error for the N3 sample is 12% while the smallest is 1.9% with a bulk of the errors
 on the order of 10%. For the N11 sample, the largest error is 9% and smallest is 0.4% with a
 bulk of the errors on the order of 4%. The largest error for the N24 sample is 12% and the smallest
 is 0.6% with a bulk of the errors being around 4%. The N24 sample mass has a very large
 measurement error as detailed earlier and it is no surprise that most of the calculated results
 manage to be within that large error. The largest error for the three samples is 16% for the 116
 group P3P3 calculation of N24 and one again finds that this calculation has a substantial shift in

 120

 accuracy from the P3P1 calculation (9% versus 16%) which is out of place for being only
 associated with a change in the anisotropic scattering kernel. The errors range between 0.01 to
 0.02 pcm which are small for the typical eigenvalue calculation of interest for DIF3D.

 12.3 ZPPR-15D Doppler Sample Reactivity Worth

 The last set of Doppler sample measurements was done in loading 207 of ZPPR-15D.
 As stated earlier, all four Doppler samples (N3, N11, N24 and E33-A) were measured in this
 loading. While loading 43 and 139 both contain Pu dominated fuel forms, loading 207 is an
 enriched uranium fuel form (Pu is present in the loading for source multiplication needs). In
 some cases, especially the E-33A sample, the measured worth is so low that it likely is unusable
 for validation purposes as will be discussed shortly.

 The reference temperature was again 300 K and each Doppler sample measurement
 involved 5 temperature variations. Table 106, Table 107, Table 108, and Table 109 provide the
 N3, N11, N24, and E33-A Doppler sample worth results for DIF3D, respectively. The same
 space-angle-energy approximations used in the preceding results were used for these samples.
 The N03 sample has a maximum error of 9.9% and smallest error of 6.2% with the typical error
 being 7%. The N11 sample has a maximum error of 16% and minimum error of 12%. The N24
 sample has errors between 21% and 23% while the E33 sample has errors ranging between 11%

 121

 and 24%. A quick survey of all of the results indicates that they have larger errors than those
 observed in loading 43 and 139.
