 Starting with the N3 sample, the measured Doppler results are similar in magnitude to
 those taken during loading 43 and 139. For the N11 sample, the magnitude of the errors is
 consistent with that observed with loading 43 but notably higher than the loading 139 results.
 The N24 Doppler sample results in Table 108 are considerably larger than those in loading 139.
 The E33-A Doppler sample results in Table 109 are by far the worst results obtained in any of
 the loadings and samples. The average error is well above 15% with maximum errors of 24%.

 One common aspect of all of these measurements is that the errors in the calculated
 results are in the seventh significant digit of k-effective or ±0.0000001. Because the iterative
 error reported by DIF3D is typically less than 10-9 on the eigenvalue and 10-8 on the fission
 source, we should be able to rule this out as a reason for the accuracy issues. What cannot be
 ruled out as sources of error are the cross section generation methodology for the Doppler sample
 and how those cross sections are used by DIF3D.

 Because all of the other cross sections generated for the other drawers are identical
 between the base (300 K) and perturbed state (higher temperature), those can be ruled out as the
 dominate sources of error. A much more complicated cylindrical model was constructed for the
 Doppler sample in MC2-3 and it did improve the Doppler sample results but not drastically (max
 24% error dropped to 22%). What should be clear is that one can obviously manipulate the cross
 section generation scheme to manipulate the output results of DIF3D and it is difficult to argue
 which scheme is applicable in a real reactor model. In that regard, the current simple model was
 retained.

 With regards to how DIF3D uses the data, there are several things to consider. First, the
 ISOTXS file produced by MC2-3 is real precision and thus some amount of truncation error
 should occur although this is typically not a source of error. To check it, the cross section
 difference between the 300K and 1083K temperatures of the E33 sample were compared and the
 maximum difference was 10% in a few groups for U-235 fission and 39% in the U-238 (n,γ)
 reaction for a few groups. These large changes in the cross section values should dominate the
 actual worth and they would not be impacted by the real precision storage of the cross section
 data.

 Another aspect to consider is how DIF3D uses the cross section data. Because the worth
 is small (less than 0.4 pcm), the first order perturbation theory option of PERSENT was used to

 123

get the Doppler sample results and it was found to produce much worse errors (typically double
the direct calculation) indicating that the changes in the flux solution cannot be ignored. Because
those changes only impact the zones that were perturbed, this means the flux solution changes
in the Doppler sample meshes cannot be ignored when it comes to calculating the worth. This is
a very unusual outcome of a worth calculation that is this small. In that regard, another aspect
that cannot be eliminated as a source of error is that DIF3D uses a compositional χ treatment.
This treatment has a non-uniform impact on the fission source part of the operator and might be
a source of error for this type of small worth calculation. Normally this should not contribute
much to the overall error, but it cannot be ruled out because the first order result did so poorly.

 Other potential sources of error are the fact that the Doppler samples do not report the U-
236 or U-234 content while several of the uranium plates in ZPPR do have these details. One
would again not normally consider this to be a substantial issue, however, given the small worth
measured for this sample, such mistakes can impact the overall calculated results compared
against the experimental measurements. Because of the errors made by the experimenters in
reporting the equipment being used and the measurement details, this set of Doppler sample
measurements should not be considered the gold standard for software validation. Even with the
stated issues, using the assumptions made about the samples and equipment results in DIF3D
producing results that are resonably accurate with respect to the measured sample worths.

12.4 Modifications to the Doppler Sample Loadings

 For all of the Doppler sample measurements in ZPPR-15A, ZPPR-15B, and ZPPR-15D,
only a single sample drawer was present and thus the measured reactivity worth was small (<1
pcm). None of these sample measurements are directly solvable with MCNP (the perturbation
theory option of MCNP might be usable but was not tested). Consequently, this section focuses
on creating Doppler sample loadings that allow the MCNP code to produce a comparable result
and assessing the DIF3D methodology in that regard. A hypothetical loading is created such that
63 additional sample drawers were introduced into each loading (thus 64 total sample drawers
were modeled). Figure 80 shows an example modification of loading 43 of ZPPR-15A, termed
loading 43mod63 where the modification to ZPPR-15B (139mod63) and ZPPR-15D
(207mod63) are similar in concept and not shown for brevity. All of the DIF3D calculations in
this section are done with a 116 energy group structure and a P 3 angular flux and P3 scattering
kernel.

 Because the sample drawer does not contain fuel, or it is not similar to the core drawer
that was replaced, this leads to a severe reduction in criticality compared with the actual
experimental loading. All 64 sample drawers were setup to have the same geometry and
composition. As was done for the axial expansion case, there is no experimental basis for these
hypothetical loadings and MCNP is assumed to produce the reference solution given it has the
best representation of the underlying geometry. To eliminate problems with evaluated cross
section libraries in MCNP and to get a large enough Doppler reactivity worth, a single
temperature change from 300 K to 1200 K was calculated for all samples in the hypothetical
loadings.

 124

 Figure 80. Radial Core Configuration of Loading 43 and Loading 43mod63 of ZPPR-15A

 To begin, Table 100 indicated that the total sample worth had some nominal amount of
error although the details of the experiment make the DIF3D comparison rather suspect. To
verify this, the total sample worth for the modified loading cases was solved using MCNP and
DIF3D and the results are tabulated in Table 110.

sample worth for all samples in all loadings. With the exception of loading 207mod63, the
sample worth comparison between DIF3D and MCNP is exceptionally good. In the higher error
cases of the N3, N11, and N24 samples, this problem is believed to be a result of differing
spectrum between the modified loadings 43 and 139 and loading 207. It is noted that the enriched
sample in this case is very accurate with DIF3D whereas the depleted uranium samples all have
high errors. Based upon the other results, this is most likely a shortcoming in the Doppler sample
cross section generation methodology constructed for these loadings.

of the Doppler samples is as good as it can be assuming that the modeled experiment is consistent
with the real experiment.
13.Analysis of TLD Loadings
 There are four ZPPR-15 experiments that covered TLD measurements: ZPPR-15B
Loading 115, 122 and 133 and ZPPR-15D Loading 202. Problems with the gamma related
solutions for EBR-II, ZPPR, and other reactors led to the identification of mistakes in the
processing of the ENDF cross section data that was available in MC 2. This section discusses the
analysis of TLD measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 115 and 133 and in ZPPR-15D Loading
202 using the recently updated MC2 gamma library.

13.1 Background on TLD Measurements

 A thermoluminescent dosimeter (TLD) consists of a thermoluminescent material in a
capsule. The thermoluminescent material contains a number of electron or hole traps which are
stable at room temperature. Some of these traps are filled when ionizing radiation creates
electrons or holes in the thermoluminescent material. When the irradiated thermoluminescent
material is subsequently heated, the electrons or holes are released from the trapping centers by
thermal ionization. Ionized carriers move through the thermoluminescent material until electron-
hole annihilation occurs. This annihilation is accompanied by emission of a photon.

 126

Measurement of the number and intensity of these photons provides an estimate of the radiation
dose received by the thermoluminescent material during irradiation.

 There are several thermoluminescent materials and various possible capsule materials.
Stainless-steel encapsulated 7LiF thermoluminescent dosimeters were used to measure gamma
doses as part of the ZPPR-15 experiments [26]. The Li in the LiF was enriched to approximately
99.993% Li-7 because neutron reactions with Li-6 obscure the gamma contribution to the
measured dose. For convenience, TLD will refer either to the LiF thermoluminescent material
inside the capsule or to the LiF material plus the stainless steel capsule in this report.

 Two types of dosimeters were used in the ZPPR-15 experiments. The first type was a
stainless steel cylindrical capsule with a diameter of 12.7 mm and height of 6.35 mm which could
contain up to five LiF disks. Two LiF disks were placed in each capsule for the ZPPR
experiments. The recorded measurement was the average of these two LiF disks. The dimensions
of the LiF disks were not listed in available reports, but for realistic capsule wall thicknesses, it
is likely that each LiF disk was a circular disk or chip with a diameter of ~10 mm and a thickness
of ~0.5 mm. These TLDs were placed in cavities in special tunnel sodium plates, i.e., sodium
plates with a 12.7 mm cavity, which replaced normal sodium plates at selected locations in the
assembly. These TLDs were used in the core and blanket.

 The second type of dosimeter was a stainless-steel clad cylindrical capsule with an outer
diameter of 3.175 mm and height of 12.7 mm which contained one LiF rod. The rod had a 1 mm
square cross section and height of 6 mm. The thickness of the capsule wall was ~0.035 in. or
~0.889 mm, and the steel was Type 304 stainless steel. The second type of dosimeter was
sandwiched between normal material plates for the gamma measurements.

 Type 304 stainless steel was widely used at ZPPR-15, so it is likely that the capsule for
the first type of dosimeter also consisted of Type 304 stainless steel. The capsule wall thickness
was a very important parameter for TLD measurements, so it is probable that the capsule wall
thickness for the first type of dosimeter also was ~0.889 mm.

13.2 Analysis of TLD Measurements in Loadings 115 and 133 of ZPPR-15B and in Loading
    202 of ZPPR-15D

 The TLD irradiations were performed such that the integrated reactor power was about
300 watt-hours, and integrated counts from the ex-core BF3 detectors provided power
normalization. Dosimeters were removed from the assembly 20 hours after reactor shutdown.

 The TLDs for the gamma dose distribution measurement were located in half 1. Figure
81 to Figure 83 show the matrix maps for half 1 of ZPPR-15B loading 115 and 133 and ZPPR-
15D loading 202; the directions along which TLD measurements were performed are indicated
with a dashed line. In Figure 82, the layout of loading 133 is cut at column 73 (reflector), however
the TLD measurements in the X direction extended up to column 76 in the reflector. The detailed
location of the TLDs in each ZPPR loading and the DIF3D model are provided in Appendix D4.

 127

Figure 81. Directions of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 115

 128

 Figure 82. Direction of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15B Loading 133

 129

 Figure 83. Direction of TLD Measurements in ZPPR-15D Loading 202

 For the calculations of the gamma dose rates, MC 2 was used to produce both neutron and
gamma cross section files. As implied from the preceding description, the TLD measurements
are provided as absolute measurements. Doing a direct comparison with DIF3D would require
applying the same power level but this introduces many sources of error from just the power
normalization of DIF3D/MC2 itself into the comparison. Thus, for the comparison work here,
two normalization schemes are considered: a) set the total measured and calculated gamma dose
rates in the active core of the X axis measurement to be equivalent and b) set the total measured

 130

and calculated does rates in the experiment equal to each other. This converts the absolute
measured TLD data into a relative comparison which is similar to what was done with the
spectral indices measurements provided earlier in this report. Neutron cross sections are
processed in 230 groups while the gamma cross sections are obtained in 21 groups. Calculated
values were previously obtained with both gamma libraries in 21 and 94 energy groups and the
results were practically identical and thus only the 21 group results are presented here. Because
the data is converted to a relative comparison, there should be no real differences in the two
libraries resulting from the data library correction but the refined energy group structure should
indicate any lack of fidelity in the coarser one.

 While the neutron cross sections of fissile zones are generated in slab geometry to
account for the plate heterogeneity of the drawer, the gamma cross section files only link to those
neutron cross sections and are not flux weighted in the same manner. The GAMSOR code [11]
is used to obtain both the neutron and gamma flux distributions in the ZPPR-15 experiments.
GAMSOR consists of three steps: 1) calculate the neutron flux distribution, 2) obtain the gamma
source distribution from the neutron reactions, 3) obtain the gamma flux distribution.

 The measurements are given in neutron dose rate of rad/s which by unit conversion
translates into Joule/kg. The MC2-3 cross section output of interest is the gamma heating data
stored in eV-barns in the PMATRX file. The typical use is to use this to get power delivered in
the domain and thus the cross section is multiplied by the atom density and a conversion factor
between eV and Joules to get Joules/cm. Multiplying by the flux will give Watts/cm 3. As stated,
the experiment is irradiated over a specified time and the volume of the TLD is known.
Multiplying by those two quantities will give the energy deposited as Joules. To convert to the
units of the experiment, we need to know the mass of the TLD material in kg. Because the exact
mass and volume of the TLDs used in the experiment are unknown, it is safe to assume that they
are all equivalent such that this aspect of the calculation cancels out. We therefore take the
gamma heating cross section, the conversion factors from eV to Joule and barn to cm 2, and
multiply by the gamma flux at the TLD centroid to get Joule/s. The DIF3D utility program
EvaluateFlux is used to evaluate the flux solution at the exact centroid of each TLD and the
heating data from the PMATRX file is converted into an ISOTXS file usable by EvaluateFlux
such that it will produce the isotopic evaluation at each selected point.

 Table 112 shows the comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the X-
axis of ZPPR-15B loading 115 while Table 113 shows the comparison along the Y-axis. The
comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the X- and Y-axis of ZPPR-15B
loading 115 is also plotted in Figure 84 and Figure 85, respectively. Besides the centroid location
of each TLD, the tables provide additional details on the drawer master associated with the
measurements where the schematic view of each drawer is available in Appendix C. The
experimental uncertainties listed in Table 112 and Table 113 are the statistical uncertainties of
the measurements and not the total uncertainties for the TLD measurements. As the calculated
results are based upon gamma heating data that is not adjusted for isotopic composition in MC 2
and the actual measurement error is considerable on the TLD measurements, as detailed in [26],
high error results are not unexpected for these measurements.

 131

 The N+G column in Table 112 refers to combining the neutron and gamma dose together.
As detailed earlier, the TLD are somewhat sensitive to neutron interactions > 1 MeV and while
this column merges the entire neutron dose with the gamma dose (and applying normalization
A), it indicates that the spatial distribution is not really impacted if the total neutron dose rate is
included. Thus, there would have to be a very strong spatial dependence of the neutron dose rate
from those fast neutrons in order for this to be a dominate corrective factor in the results. As the
results displayed are rather good in the active core and poor in the blanket (501, 502,…) and
reflector (401, 402, …), one can assume the same problem exists as was observed with the earlier
foil measurements in the blanket and reflector regions although as mentioned, the gamma heating
data is not adjusted in the MC2 calculation process as it was in the foil data.

 In Figure 84 and Figure 85, the experimental dose rate values are indexed to the left axis
while the error for the two normalization schemes are indexed to the right axis. The first figure
also indicates the reactor zone where the measurements are performed. The comparison shows
that the calculated gamma dose rates agree rather well with the experimental values in the active
core region with notable increases in error in the outer core region. They also indicate that the
two normalization schemes are effectively identical.

Figure 85. Calculated and Measured Gamma Dose Rates Along the Y-axis of ZPPR-15B L115

 Table 115 shows the comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the X-
axis of ZPPR-15B loading 122. The tables are setup similarly to those above and the
experimental uncertainties are the statistical uncertainties of the measurements and not the total
uncertainties for the TLD measurements. The comparison of the measured and errors in the
calculated dose rates of ZPPR-15B loading 122 for two normalization schemes are plotted in
Figure 86. As was the case with L115, only the 21 energy group results are presented here. The
two schemes for normalization have very little impact on the total error and it is rather obvious
that the blanket, reflector, sodium pool, and shielding regions all have high errors. With the
addition of the simulated sodium pool region, the calculated results are particularly bad in that
region but the magnitude of the measured value is also miniscule. Comparing the outer core
errors to those of L115, one finds they have similar magnitudes of error indicating that the
methodology (or cross section data) are leading to the same behavior in the error distribution.

 Table 115 shows the comparison of the measured and calculated dose rates along the X-
axis of ZPPR-15B loading 133. The tables are setup similarly to those above. The comparison
of the measured and errors for the calculated dose rates along the X-axis of ZPPR-15B loading
133 for two normalization schemes is plotted in Figure 87. As was the case with L115 and L122,
the experimental uncertainties listed in Table 115 are the statistical uncertainties of the
measurements and not the total uncertainties for the TLD measurements and only the 21 group
results are presented here. The calculated results have a reasonable agreement in the outer core
region but are notably off in the blanket, reflector, and shielding regions consistent with L115
and L122.

Figure 86. Calculated and Measured Gamma Dose Rates Along the X-axis of ZPPR-15B L122

Figure 87. Calculated and Measured Gamma Dose Rates Along the X-axis of ZPPR-15B L133

 As is quite evident in Table 116 and Table 117, the errors in the active core region are
considerably higher than they were in the ZPPR-15B measurements for the same regions. There
is no particular trend other than that drawer 946 through 948 have considerably higher error than

 136

the surrounding drawers. Inspection of the drawers shows that these are the drawers with Zr
included in them while the surrounding drawers (945 and 944) do not have Zr in them. Of course
the central drawers 949 all have Zr in them and the error is considerably less. Both Figure 88
and Figure 89 show the same trend occurs in the X and Y direction. It is worth noting that the
experimental measurements have a considerable drop from ~1.0 to ~0.6 in drawers 946 through
948 and a considerable rise back to ~0.8 and ~1.0 as you move further out in the active core. In
the calculated value, the same drawers show a lesser decrease and it is not clear from the
materials involved what would be the fundamental reason for a large drop off in the experimental
or calculated values.

Figure 88. Calculated and Measured Gamma Dose Rates Along the X-axis of ZPPR-15D L202

Figure 89. Calculated and Measured Gamma Dose Rates Along the Y-axis of ZPPR-15D L202

 139

 An interesting aspect to note in the preceding TLD calculations is the lack of a clear
pattern in the experimental measurement data. For the spectral indices, the reaction rates
generally are high at the core center and drop down with the transition to the outer core, blanket,
and reflector regions. The exception of course were the U-238 (n,f) measurements which are
threshold based and thus sensitive to the fast neutron flux throughout the core. For the TLD,
there are notable peaks in specific drawers and when compared against the U-238 (n,f) reaction
rate curve in Figure 50 one finds a similar peaking behavior. This outcome is actually consistent
with the known behavior of the TLDs which had a sensitivity to fast neutrons that is not captured
in the present analysis as only gamma heating is considered. The fast neutron sensitivity is easy
to incorporate in the analysis but the relative magnitude of its importance to the gamma heating
was not explicitly defined in the ZPPR literature and thus no further work was taken here to
correct the obvious issues that are occurring.

 In light of the fast neutron sensitivity, one should be able to understand that the TLD
measurements actually have a considerably larger error than that defined by the statistical error
provided by the experimenters. This aspect does not reduce the importance of the TLD
measurements as these are some of the only clean direct measurements available today of gamma
heating from a critical fission system. Thus while the TLD measurements might have one-sigma
errors as high as 20%, the fact that the deterministic code produces a good comparison to the
overall distribution is sufficient for the fast reactor usage as gamma heating is only ~5% of the
total energy released in the system. It is noted that the error in the predictions could be
substantially lower than that inferred from the preceding TLD results because of the sensitivity
to fast neutrons.
14.Conclusions
 This preceding results covered the calculated results of many different experimental
measurements carried out in ZPPR-15. These experiments were identified to be particularly
important for use as validation data of the reactivity coefficients generated by MC 2+DIF3D.
Those reactivity coefficients are used in the safety analysis work in fast reactor development
projects. The projection of each experimental measurement to a given reactivity coefficient is
beyond the scope of this manuscript. Thus, this manuscript is focused on just the methodology
used to create the model in the ARC software and the accuracy of the calculated results with
respect to the measurement. Additional results using MCNP are also provided as was possible.

 At the beginning of this manuscript, a discussion of the ZPPR facility and the ZPPR-15
experiments was given. The methodology for constructing models with the MC 2+DIF3D
software of ARC was displayed and discussed along with the impact of different approximations
in the deterministic code DIF3D. The first task of the analysis work was to verify that the
criticality predictions were reliable. As discussed, both MCNP and MC2+DIF3D were found to
produce the typical errors in criticality for ZPPR-15A and -15B. For 15C and 15D, the errors
were notably higher and likely a problem with the multi-group cross section processing
methodology as MCNP did not display the same issues. Thus while the DIF3D results for 15C
and 15D are less accurate than MCNP, they sufficiently reliable for the reactivity worth
calculations that the various ZPPR experiments entail.

 140

 The simulated control rod measurements were discussed first after the assessment of the
critical loadings was completed. In the control rod experiments, the accuracy of the calculation
relies upon the ability of the software to accurately and consistently predict the criticality of
different, but similar, loadings of the ZPPR reactor. In most of the control rod worth calculations
both MCNP and MC2+DIF3D performed well with errors less than 6% with respect to the
measurements.

 The void worth measurements are similar to the simulated control rod measurements in
that they require accurate and consistent prediction of criticality for consecutive loadings of the
ZPPR reactor. Unlike the control rod results, the void worth calculations varied considerably
from small errors <<10% to large errors ranging from 20-60%. In most cases, the larger errors
are associated with small void worth measurements for which MCNP was also found to have
difficulties. MC2+DIF3D can be viewed as less accurate than MCNP and non-trivial errors
occurred on large reactivity measurements for both MCNP and MC2+DIF3D. The accuracy
issues with MC2+DIF3D point to problems with the cross section processing and modeling
methodology and might be resolved with further analysis effort but in the end, the MCNP result
would be expected and for many of the measurements those results would still have large errors.
The existing set of sodium void measurements should be sufficient for most fast reactor
development projects for this fuel type but some effort has to be taken to exclude the smaller
void measurements with large errors as the measurement itself is somewhat suspect.

 The neutron spectrum measurements showed good results in the core region and
relatively poor performance in the special shielding region. This last aspect indicates that the
predictive capabilities of the MCNP and MC 2+DIF3D software degrade in the outer shielding
parts of the reactor and more measurement data would be wise. One aspect that was important
to note was that the fast flux predictions from MCNP and MC 2+DIF3D were high indicating that
the neutron damage to materials could be consistently over predicted compared with
experiments. This work indicates that alternative shielding specific experimental measurements
should be identified for the fast reactor design work.

 The B-10 counter measurements had significant errors for both MCNP and MC 2+DIF3D
where MCNP was generally better. As was the case with the neutron spectrum measurements,
the accuracy generally degraded as the measurement moved from the core into the special
shielding region.

 The foil measurements were calculated with MC 2+DIF3D as the modeling foils with
MCNP is rather impractical. MC2+DIF3D produced excellent results for most of the foils in the
active core and surrounding blanket region. In the surrounding steel reflector and other ex-core
regions, the MC2+DIF3D foil predictions had significant errors and a similar conclusion to the
B-10 counter measurements applies.

 The Pu-242 and Np237 measurements could not be analyzed with MCNP due to the
modeling limitations and only MC2+DIF3D results are available. The MC2+DIF3D results were
notably inaccurate as the measurements were taken in the ex-core regions. Much like the foil and
spectrum measurements in these regions, the MC 2+DIF3D results indicate that the predictive
capability in the ex-core regions is less reliable than in the core.

 141

 The axial expansion results could not be analyzed with MCNP due to the very small
worth (< 1 pcm). The MC2+DIF3D work investigated many different models due to the
underlying difficulty in modeling the actual experiment. Using the most reliable modeling
methodology, the errors in the small reactivity measurements were less than 10% for the
transport approximation with several of the results within the one-sigma error bars on the
measurements.

 The Doppler worth of heated samples could not be analyzed with MCNP due to the very
small worth (< 1 pcm). Unlike the axial expansion results, the MC 2+DIF3D results were
generally good to excellent with errors less than 15% for most samples. Manufactured problems
were again constructed so that MCNP could obtain a solution and when compared with
MC2+DIF3D, they were generally in agreement with < 12% error.

 Finally, the results of three of four TLD measurements was presented. The general trend
of the results was good showing a good comparison in the gamma heating between DIF3D and
the experiment. However, there were several TLD locations where the errors are notably larger
than adjacent drawer measurements which are difficult to explain. The most likely issue is the
sensitivity of the TLD to fast neutrons which is not easy to assess as the ZPPR experimenters
failed to quantify the magnitude of the sensitivity. It is important to note that the TLD
measurements are some of the few available fission emitted gamma related measurements for
reactor physics available for a fast reactors.

 Overall, most of the experiments shown will be excellent candidates for validation data.
Additional work is required to assess how consistent these measurements are with any fast
reactor project and what error is introduced by the inconsistencies. While some of the calculated
results indicate that additional work should be taken for MC 2+DIF3D, that work is assumed to
be included in a revision of this report in the future as deemed necessary.

18.Appendix C. Drawer Mapping Specifications used in BuildZPRModel
Figure 161 provides the color index for all pictorial representations of the drawer types used in
ZPPR-15 included in Table 123. The cross section mapping details used in BuildZPRmodel for
all loadings is included for ZPPR-15A in Table 124, for ZPPR-15B in Table 125, and for
ZPPR-15D in Table 126. The pictorial representations of each drawer only indicate the
materials present axially at the half height of the drawer. In most cases it is easy to identify the
plutonium, clad uranium, clad sodium, and stainless steel plates that were common to ZPPR-
15. The mapping decisions can generally be simply deduced by comparison of the provided
drawer pictures involved in the mapping where the general goal was to closely match the plate
loading between the two drawers.

 Figure 161. Legend for Simple Representation of all Drawer Types of ZPPR-15

 241

19.Appendix D. Exact Foil (L123, L134 and L202) and TLD (L115, L133 and L202)
 Locations
 The drawer interior was 2 inch wide and was divided into 16 columns for recording purposes
in ZPPR where Figure 162 displays an example plate loaded 2 inch section (drawer type 201
from ZPPR-15). As can be seen, the drawer is segmented into 0.125 inch pieces and a letter A-
P is assigned to each segment. Because the foils were placed on the surfaces of any given plate,
the foil location would be specified as AB, BC, CD, etc… With this methodology, for the
example in Figure 162 (drawer type 201 for ZPPR-15), foils could only be placed at the AB, CD,
GH, IJ, MN, and OP locations. Because there are two plate boundaries between H and I, the plate
interfaces were designated HH or II depending upon which side of the central plate the foil was
placed on. It was common practice to have a symmetric placement of foils around the core center
and thus on one side of the reactor the foil might be placed at IJ while the other side it is placed
at GH.

 Figure 162. Foil Boundary Locations in ZPPR
 The two letters shown in column 5 of Table 127 to Table 141 are the columns that either
contain or bound the foil or TLD location. For example, JK means that the foil/TLD was
positioned in or between columns J and K. The X-, Y- and Z-coordinates shown in all tables are
the coordinates of the foil/TLD location relative to an origin at the lower left corner of the
interface between matrix halves. Z = 0.0 is at the interface, and the positive Z-axis is the axis of
the matrix tubes in half 1. X = 0.0 is at the left side of column 11 in half 1, and Y = 0.0 is at the
bottom of row 187. Consequently, the origin corresponds to the position at the lower left corner
of the interface between half 1 and half 2 for an observer facing half 1. Table 127 to Table 141
also show the location of the mapping foils or TLDs in the DIF3D geometry model generated by
BuildZPRModel. In the DIF3D model, the rows and columns of the matrix loading map that are
made of just empty tubes are eliminated. The origin of the X and Y axes in the DIF3D model is

 242

also set at the lower left corner of the interface between half 1 and half 2 for an observer facing
half 1. However, the origin of the Z axis in the DIF3D model is at top end of half 1 (i.e., in the
DIF3D model the interface between half 1 and half 2 is located at Z= 107.32008 cm. All
dimensions in Table 127 to Table 141 are in centimeter units.
